## Summary of Issues and Major Conclusions

- BGE has listened to customers and stakeholders and, in response to affordability concerns expressed, made a deliberate decision to delay its previously announced plan to file a rate case in early 2026 in order to evaluate potential alternatives. As part of that evaluation, BGE carefully assessed the level of revenue and investment necessary to maintain its system in a safe and reliable manner. The alternatives considered included not filing a rate case at all; however, for the reasons discussed herein—including the inability to continue funding the investments and operating costs necessary to sustain a safe and reliable system—this option was not viable. Accordingly, BGE is proceeding with this filing primarily to ensure it can maintain the integrity of its system and continue providing safe, reliable service to its customers. At the same time, BGE is advancing affordability in meaningful and measurable ways, including the proposed BGE FlexPay program and an acceleration of the Limited Income Mechanism to reach the most vulnerable customers sooner. In parallel, as discussed by Company Witnesses Michael J. Cloyd and Laura A. Wright, BGE continues to exercise rigorous financial and operational discipline, delivering customer benefits through efficiency initiatives that have helped control costs. BGE's request allows for the investments necessary to maintain the safety and reliability of the electric distribution system for Central Maryland and for BGE's customers—reliability which has taken decades to achieve. Thus, in this historic test year request, BGE has weighed its primary obligation to provide safe and reliable service with customer affordability, focusing on the electric infrastructure investments that are the most essential to continued safe and reliable service, while also being mindful of customer bills. As Company Witness Wright explains further, the consequences of reducing and deferring critical infrastructure investment right now puts the reliability of the system customers depend on each and every day at risk of degradation, leading to more frequent and prolonged service outages for customers with even greater costs for BGE and customers over time since emergency repairs are more costly for customers than planned replacements (and the cost of a repair in the future will cost more).
- Just as the obligation BGE has to provide safe and reliable service to customers does not pause during periods of economic difficulty, the Commission's role under the regulatory compact likewise continues, including ensuring that the utility remains financially capable of fulfilling that obligation.
- In the past few years, BGE has reinvested more than 95% of its net profit back into the system, for the benefit of customers and to support affordability. This underscores our commitment to reliability, safety, and customer service – demonstrating that any profits go back into maintaining the grid that serves customers. Further, the Company's required spending to run a safe and reliable distribution system is far higher than its profit. That is why even after reinvesting earnings, we rely on debt and equity to fund the remaining necessary spend – all with the goal of delivering the service quality, safety, and reliability that customers and regulators expect.
- In fact, the portion of the average residential BGE bill driven by State-imposed items (EmPOWER MD, taxes and other fees) is three times more than the portion that is due to BGE electric distribution profits.
- BGE FlexPay is a proposed prepaid program, paired with an arrearage management component, offering customers greater control, flexibility, and visibility into their energy usage and bills, building on a previously successful pilot. BGE is seeking approval of a full rollout of BGE FlexPay because it has heard customers and understands they are facing pressure from rising energy bills, primarily driven by electric supply costs. While many of the cost pressures on a bill are not within the Company's control, BGE recognizes that customers experience one BGE bill, and we are focused on providing tools that empower customers to manage their energy usage and giving them more control over that bill in real time.
- For BGE to continue responding effectively and efficiently to storm events and maintain the restoration experience to which customers have become accustomed, the Company is requesting a rider to provide for more timely funding of restoration resources and to allow for storm event expenses to be more gradually reflected on customer bills. Importantly, this mechanism protects customers by ensuring that BGE recovers no more and no less than the actual level of incremental outage event expense for all sizes of storms. Differences between the level of restoration expenses actually incurred and restoration expenses authorized as the baseline in rates would be deferred to a regulatory asset or liability and charged/refunded to customers under the proposed Storm Restoration Expense Rider.
- In response to Commission direction in Order No. 90948 in Case No. 9692, BGE is providing a benefit-cost analysis of the 2023 Conduit Agreement, demonstrating that the agreement clearly delivers more measurable benefits than costs to customers. This analysis and testimony also supports the ratemaking adjustments proposed to begin recovering the conduit regulatory asset permitted by the Commission in Order No. 92106.
- In this proceeding, BGE is requesting an increase in electric base distribution revenues of $156.1 million based on a historic test year of April 1, 2025, through March 31, 2026, adjusted for known and measurable changes during the rate-effective period. If granted in full, the requested rate adjustment would result in an overall increase of 2.8 percent in total electric bills, but the impact will vary from rate schedule to rate schedule and from customer to customer. The total monthly bill for a residential electric customer with average monthly usage of 851 kWh is expected to increase by approximately 4 percent (or about $8 per month). With the proposed increase, the electric distribution cost (delivery cost) would comprise approximately 30 percent (approximately $60) of the total electric bill.
- Consistent with adjustments approved by the Commission in numerous prior historic test year rate case proceedings, the Company is also including ratemaking adjustments in this proceeding to include certain safety and reliability investments in rate base and operating income on a terminal basis. These known and measurable adjustments are warranted for this portfolio of investments, a portfolio which is necessary to maintain the safety and reliability of BGE's electric distribution infrastructure.
- The explicit approvals being sought from the Commission, including the items noted above, are summarized in the list below and also at the end of this Direct Testimony:[^1]

  1. A rate of return for its electric operations of 7.68% as proposed by Company Witness Cloyd, based on the Company's 4.72% embedded cost of debt, as well as the 10.4% ROE for electric, as recommended by Company Witness Adrien M. McKenzie;
  2. Recovery of incremental major outage event expenses which were incurred in August 2023 and February 2025, with recovery over a five-year period consistent with Commission Order No. 89678 as provided in Operating Income Adjustment 20 and Rate Base Adjustment 7. The incremental cost of each event has been deferred in a regulatory asset pursuant to Case No. 9645, Order No. 89678;
  3. Recovery of incremental 2023 minor outage event expenses over a five-year period consistent with Commission Order No. 92106 and inclusion of the regulatory asset in rate base (Operating Income Adjustment 21 and Rate Base Adjustment 8). The 2023 incremental minor outage event expenses have been deferred in a regulatory asset pursuant to Order No. 92106 in Case No. 9645;
  4. Recovery of the 2023 conduit costs over a five-year period (Operating Income Adjustment 22 and Rate Base Adjustment 9) consistent with the existing conduit contract term and inclusion of this regulatory asset in rate base given that the 2023 [continued below]

## I. Qualifications

**Q. Please state your name and business address.**

A. My name is John C. Frain, and my business address is Baltimore Gas and Electric Company ("BGE" or the "Company"), 2 Center Plaza, 110 West Fayette Street, Baltimore, Maryland, 21201.

**Q. What is your position with BGE?**

A. I am the Vice President of Regulatory Policy and Strategy. In this capacity I am responsible for the following functions: regulatory administration and pricing including overseeing ratemaking activities at the Maryland Public Service Commission (the "Commission") and Federal Energy Regulatory Commission ("FERC"); strategy and clean energy programs such as battery storage, energy efficiency and demand response; electric and gas energy supply; regional grid operator PJM Interconnection, L.L.C. ("PJM") load settlement and wholesale market interfaces; and customer choice programs.

**Q. Please describe your educational background.**

A. I hold a Bachelor of Science Degree in Business Administration with a Major in Accounting from the University of Richmond in Virginia and a Master of Business Administration from Loyola University in Maryland. I also was awarded a Certified Public Accountant license in Maryland.

**Q. Please describe your business experience.**

A. I have been employed by the Company since 2005, serving previously as the Director of Regulatory Strategy and Revenue Policy and in various other capacities in Finance & Accounting at BGE. Before coming to BGE, I was employed as an auditor at Arthur Andersen.

**Q. Are you involved with any civic organizations?**

A. Yes, I am. I serve as an elder at Hunt Valley Church and am on the Boards of Directors of Meals on Wheels of Central Maryland as well as the Baltimore Symphony Orchestra. I have previously served on the Board of Trustees for Cambridge School as Secretary and as Treasurer as well as the Adult Committee for Young Life Baltimore County.

**Q. Have you previously testified before the Maryland Public Service Commission?**

A. Yes. See Company Exhibit JCF-1 for a summary of my testimony experience in formal proceedings. I have also appeared before the Commission at its weekly Administrative Meetings and legislative-style hearings.

## II. Purpose of Testimony

**Q. What is the purpose of your testimony?**

A. There are two parts to my Direct Testimony. Part I of my testimony provides an overview of BGE's business, introduces the Company witnesses participating in this proceeding, explains BGE's requested electric revenue adjustment, and addresses BGE's focus on customer affordability and the community it serves, including a proposed prepaid service offering for residential customers called BGE FlexPay. Part I of my testimony also supports a proposed cost recovery mechanism for incremental storm operations and maintenance ("O&M") expenses and provides the benefit-cost analysis related to the 2023 Conduit Agreement that BGE was directed to perform by the Commission.

Part II of my Direct Testimony discusses BGE's requested revenue adjustment and certain ratemaking adjustments reflected in the revenue requirement supported by my revenue requirement exhibits, which I will walk through in Part II of my testimony. Part II of my testimony also includes a review of historic capital additions, further supported by Company Witnesses Cloyd and Wright, and test year O&M expenses.

## Part I – Policy Testimony

**Q. What is the purpose of Part I of your Direct Testimony?**

A. Part I of my Direct Testimony is offered in support of the electric base rate increase sought by BGE.

1. First, I will briefly describe the Company's overall business.
2. Second, I will introduce the other witnesses for the Company who will participate in this proceeding and briefly describe the nature of their testimony.
3. Third, I will set forth BGE's requested electric revenue adjustment and discuss why it is necessary given the Company's investments in its electric distribution system. I will also discuss the expected impact on customer bills.
4. Fourth, I will discuss the programs and tools the Company offers today and is proposing to offer its customers to assist with rising electricity costs and address affordability challenges. This includes an overview of the various charges on a residential energy bill, and which charges are directly controlled by and paid to BGE.
5. Fifth, I will provide an overview of the Company's commitment to supporting the communities it serves, beyond providing utility service, through BGE's workforce development programs, which aim to build and empower a workforce that represents the communities we serve. I will also discuss BGE's industry honors and recognition through the engagements and contributions that BGE makes to the many communities it serves across Central Maryland.
6. Sixth, I will provide an overview of BGE FlexPay, a prepaid service program that the Company is proposing in this filing to benefit customers.
7. Seventh, I will provide an overview of a proposed recovery mechanism for incremental storm O&M costs.
8. Eighth, I will discuss the results of a benefit cost analysis ("BCA") BGE was directed to perform by the Commission for the 2023 Conduit Agreement, in support of the ratemaking adjustments discussed in Part II of my Direct Testimony which are necessary for recovering the conduit regulatory asset approved by the Commission in Order No. 92106 concerning the 2023 Final Reconciliation in Case No. 9645.

**Q. Are you sponsoring any exhibits in Part I of your Direct Testimony?**

A. Yes. In addition to Company Exhibit JCF-1 mentioned above, I have prepared the following Company Exhibits:

- Company Exhibit JCF-2A is related to BGE FlexPay, the Company's proposed prepaid service program, and includes a list of certain billing-related provisions from BGE's Retail Electric Service Tariff (the "Electric Tariff"), the Code of Maryland Regulations ("COMAR"), and other Commission requirements for which BGE would be seeking a waiver in order to offer BGE FlexPay.
- Company Exhibit JCF-2B is also related to BGE FlexPay and provides a copy of the Terms and Conditions for the Prepaid Pilot launched by BGE in 2019, which the Company will update based on approval of this BGE FlexPay program.
- Company Exhibit JCF-3 provides the BCA for the 2023 Conduit Agreement required by the Commission, which was prepared by an independent consultant, West Monroe Partners LLC.

## III. Overview of Baltimore Gas and Electric Company

**Q. Mr. Frain, please describe BGE's business.**

A. BGE was founded in 1816. More than two hundred years later, the Company has evolved into an electric transmission, electric distribution and gas distribution company. Since 2012, BGE has been a wholly-owned subsidiary of the Exelon Corporation ("Exelon"). BGE serves more than 1.3 million electric customers, of which approximately 90% are residential customers, across the Company's central Maryland service territory. BGE's electric service area of 2,300 square miles encompasses the City of Baltimore. BGE's overall service territories cover Baltimore City as well as all or parts of ten counties in central Maryland with an estimated population of more than 3.1 million people.

BGE delivers electricity to its customers over approximately 1,300 circuit miles of transmission lines and over 27,000 circuit miles of distribution lines. For customers who do not select alternative energy providers under BGE's electric retail customer choice program, BGE is also the default electricity supplier.

## V. BGE's Rate Request Is Needed to Maintain a Safe and Reliable System

**Q. Why is BGE requesting an electric rate adjustment?**

A. The Company is obligated to provide safe and reliable service to customers and requires the appropriate funding to fulfill that obligation. That said, we have heard the voices of customers and other stakeholders regarding affordability concerns and are on a journey to significantly reduce BGE's investment plan such that our future planned investments focus almost entirely on the most immediate risks to the system. We are generally holding off on system upgrades in order to minimize cost impacts to customers. However, as discussed by Company Witness Wright, BGE is submitting this filing in order to move forward with even these reduced investments, without which the condition of the system will decline, outage frequency and duration will increase, and future costs ultimately borne by customers will rise as system issues become more severe and more expensive to address.[^4] As the utility, BGE recognizes that operational needs must be balanced with affordability. As such, we do not take a rate request lightly at any time, let alone during a period of elevated costs for essentials such as food, housing, and energy. However, the obligation to provide safe and reliable service to our customers does not pause during periods of economic difficulty and the consequences of deferring critical infrastructure investment will not only impact customer service, but as Company Witness Cloyd testifies, will ultimately impose far greater costs over time.[^5] As Company Witness Wright testifies, deferred maintenance leads to more frequent and prolonged service outages, emergency repairs that are more expensive than planned replacements, and degradation of the system on which our customers depend each and every day.[^6] The Company has designed its rate request to balance these competing imperatives: making the infrastructure investments that are essential to continued safe and reliable service, while also being mindful of the impact on customer bills.

The revenue adjustment requested in this proceeding reflects the investments necessary to maintain a safe and reliable electric distribution system for our customers. This is not a historic test year request that is driven by ambitious grid modernization programs, advanced technology deployments, or large-scale resiliency initiatives. Rather, this filing is about sustaining the infrastructure that customers depend on every day. While the Company recognizes the importance of grid modernization and resilience and the long-term benefits those investments would deliver, this case is narrowly focused on the foundational investments that cannot be deferred without placing the safety and reliability of the system at risk. Simply put, this is a case about maintaining the grid, not transforming it.

**Q. Mr. Frain, can you please discuss the current regulatory landscape in Maryland and recent developments that have impacted the Company?**

A. Yes. On May 12, 2026, the Utility RELIEF (Reducing Energy Load Inflation for Everyday Families) Act was signed into law.[^7] Among other things, this legislation temporarily prohibits the approval of a rate case based on forecasted test years until the later of (1) the completion of a Commission proceeding about the use of historic and forecasted test years and (2) April 1, 2027. As a result, the test years underlying the current ratemaking process in Maryland must be, at least temporarily, historic in nature. The Company has thus prepared this base rate case application utilizing a historic test year but also urges a return to forecasted test years as a constructive form of alternative ratemaking. In the past, the historic-based ratemaking construct did not provide BGE an opportunity to earn its authorized return. As BGE has explained in prior proceedings, when rates are set based on a historic test year, capital investment spending combined with even limited increases in operating expenses and relatively flat customer growth means that a utility does not have a reasonable opportunity to earn its authorized return. Under these conditions, utilities experience a persistent and permanent regulatory lag. Even the Commission recognized regulatory lag as a shortcoming to historic test years, noting "the traditional ratemaking method does have certain perceived drawbacks such as a failure to equitably distribute risk, limited capabilities to monitor costs, limited ability to achieve policy outcomes and potential restrictions on utility innovation, and arguably regulatory lag, which can impede the utilities' ability to earn their authorized ROR."[^8]

**Q. Can you summarize how this regulatory environment is impacting the investments the Company makes to provide safe and reliable service to customers?**

A. While BGE continues to invest in its electric system, these investments are aimed at maintaining a safe and dependable electric system, as well as fulfilling the minimum regulatory and statutory requirements of an electric company in Maryland. The Company is concentrating on maintenance and meeting essential obligations, and in the interest of affordability, is not making efforts to improve system reliability. The Company is focused on investments and work that drive the level necessary to maintain the integrity of the electric system we have today.

Historically, storm restoration response has been one of the most unpredictable and most significant factors impacting BGE's maintenance and operation of the electric distribution system. To be able to continue effective response to storm events and to maintain the storm outage restoration experience customers have become accustomed to, the Company needs a mechanism for timely recovery of storm restoration expenses. Given the uncertainty of the future ratemaking environment in Maryland, this rate case represents what is necessary to sustain core operations, including an adequate means of supporting storm cost recovery through the incremental storm expense recovery mechanism proposed later in my testimony.

**Q. Please describe the Company's overall request for an electric rate adjustment in this proceeding.**

A. As set forth in my Direct Testimony and accompanying exhibits in Part II of my testimony as well as the testimony and accompanying exhibits of Company Witness McCart, BGE demonstrates the need for an increase in electric base distribution revenues of $156.1 million. On an adjusted basis, BGE earned a return for its electric distribution business during the test year ending March 31, 2026 (5.87 percent) that is 13% below the 6.77% return authorized by the Commission in Case No. 9692 for BGE's electric distribution operations. Therefore, BGE's electric base rates must be increased so that it can have a reasonable opportunity to earn the 7.68% rate of return ("ROR") supported by the Company in this proceeding. The requested rate adjustment would result in an overall increase of approximately 4% in the average residential electric bills, if the Company's requested rate adjustment is granted in toto.[^9]

**Q. What is the requested revenue adjustment based upon?**

A. BGE's requested adjustment to the electric revenue requirement is based on a historic test year for the 12 months ending March 31, 2026 (adjusted for certain known and measurable changes), a 10.40% ROE, and an equity ratio of 52%, which results in a ROR or "weighted average cost of capital" of 7.68%.

## VI. BGE Is Balancing Its Request with Additional Affordability Tools for Customers

**Q. How is customer affordability considered at BGE?**

A. BGE has heard the voices of customers, government officials, and regulators in reaction to the widespread concerns regarding increasing energy bills. We acknowledge these frustrations and concerns are real and justified, and we understand the financial hardship this presents for many individuals (including our own BGE employees, many of whom are also BGE customers), families, and businesses. We remain committed to doing our part by making prudent distribution system investment decisions that carefully consider affordability, reliability, and safety. While always the Company's approach, it is especially important now given widespread affordability concerns. We also remain committed to helping our communities during this challenging economic period through bill management and other means, and I will discuss later in my testimony ways that BGE has provided real relief to customers in need due to rising energy costs.

When a BGE customer receives their energy bill, the company name they see on the bill is BGE and, therefore, many customers naturally associate all of the charges on that bill with BGE and assume all charges leading to higher total BGE bill amounts are the result of increased costs under BGE's control. While this assumption is not correct, we understand that the overall BGE bill is what customers see and experience.

As I said before, as the utility, BGE balances affordability with our primary responsibility to operate and maintain a safe and reliable electric system for our customers. Electricity impacts customer lives today in more areas and in more important ways than ever before, leading to an ever-increasing customer dependence on the reliability of BGE's electric system. Customers' expectations are also evolving as their dependance on reliable service for daily life increases, and BGE is committed to meeting customer expectations and continuing to provide safe and reliable service to our more than 1.3 million electric customers in Maryland.

**Q. How is affordability defined?**

A. According to Merriam-Webster, affordability is "able to be afforded, having a price that is not too high" and the definition of afford is "to manage to bear without serious detriment or to be able to bear the cost of."[^10] For utility customers, as a result, affordability must then start and focus on their monthly bill, which is the very thing that customers are bearing the cost of and must manage. Certainly, other factors such as prudency, reasonableness, and cost-effectiveness can and should also be looked at by regulators and other stakeholders when evaluating certain costs and investments. But for customers, if they cannot bear or manage their monthly utility bill, none of those other factors matter. The reality is, for BGE's electric customers today, more and more of the non-distribution pieces of their utility bill, such as for electric supply and the EmPOWER Maryland program, are making it difficult to manage the cost of their monthly utility bill. Thus, when BGE focuses on affordability, it is the monthly bill that we have at the very forefront.

**Q. Mr. Frain, can you provide at a high level the various charges that appear on a residential customer's electric bill?**

A. Yes. A customer electric bill has three sections of charges: Supply, Delivery, and Taxes & Fees. Rates on the bill can be variable, meaning these charges increase when a customer uses more energy and decrease when a customer uses less energy. As a result, factors such as weather and customer behavior can impact these charges on customer bills via the amount of electricity consumed; these impacts are often heightened in the peak weather seasons of summer and winter. Examples of variable rates include the Supply Rate and the Distribution Charge. Rates can also be a fixed monthly fee, like the Customer Charge.

For the average residential electric customer, more than half of the monthly bill is attributable to their Supply charges. Figure 1 shows the current breakdown by category of charge as a proportional share of each dollar on the average residential electric customer's monthly bill.[^11] Those proportions remain largely unchanged even if the Company's proposed adjustment in this case is fully approved, assuming all else equal. Almost all, if not all, of BGE's distribution profits are reinvested back into the system for the benefit of service to customers as explained in the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Cloyd.[^12] In fact, the portion of the average residential BGE bill driven by State-imposed items (EmPOWER Maryland, taxes, and other fees) is three times more than the portion that is due to BGE electric distribution profits as shown in Figure 1 below.

Figure 1 shows the current breakdown by category of charge as a proportional share of each dollar on the average residential electric customer's monthly bill.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 1: Residential Electric Bill Breakdown at Current Rates]

A proportional breakdown chart (e.g. stacked bar or pie chart) showing how each dollar of the average residential BGE electric bill is allocated across categories, at current (pre-case) rates. Per footnote 11, the categories depicted are: (1) Delivery, which includes the Customer Charge and Distribution Charge, with the "Delivery Profit" portion of Delivery segregated out as its own visual segment; (2) Supply, which includes the Supply Charge; and (3) State of Maryland-imposed items, which includes the EmPOWER Maryland Charge, taxes, and fees. The accompanying prose establishes the key quantitative relationships the figure conveys: Supply charges make up more than half of the average monthly bill; State-imposed items (EmPOWER MD, taxes, and other fees) account for a share that is three times larger than the Delivery Profit share; and Delivery Profit is a small slice of the total bill because BGE reinvests almost all distribution profit back into the system. The figure is meant to visually rebut the perception that the "BGE" name on the bill implies most charges reflect BGE's own profit, when in fact profit is a small fraction of the total, dwarfed by supply costs (market-driven, not BGE-controlled) and state-imposed charges.

[→ See original PDF page 21 for visual rendering]

**Q. Can you provide more detail on the Supply charges that appear on a customer's electric bill?**

A. The Supply charges represent the actual physical electricity a customer uses. While BGE manages procurement processes for default supply service, the price of electricity is not primarily controlled by BGE.[^13] Recent increases in electric supply costs (driven primarily by capacity and generation prices) have been much greater than increases in distribution costs, and the associated charges on customer bills reflect this imbalance. BGE does not prescribe the supply price of electricity; that is set by market prices and fluctuates over time. Rising supply costs are a function of the electric energy market and represent a broader economic issue in Maryland. Specifically, BGE's service territory is facing a severe supply/demand imbalance as a result of decreasing energy supply due to the retirement of two critical electric generating stations in the region coupled with rising energy demand, in large part due to data centers and electrification programs. Supply costs are largely pass-through charges and, while they appear as part of a customer's overall BGE bill, they are paid to the regional grid operator, PJM, and ultimately to the power plant companies that run and operate the region's generating stations.

**Q. Can you provide more detail on the Delivery charges that appear on a residential customer's electric bill?**

A. The second section on a residential electric customer bill is the Delivery charges, which includes the Customer Charge, the Distribution Charge, and the EmPOWER Maryland Charge. The Customer Charge and Distribution Charge[^14] are the BGE residential electric delivery charges reflecting the investments made in the electric systems and the on-going O&M expenses to maintain the safety and reliability of the power grid for customers and to keep the lights on. At the same time BGE is making investments in its utility infrastructure, we have also invested back into our communities, as I will discuss later in my testimony. Together, the Customer Charge and the Distribution Charge are the BGE rates that include a component for the fair and reasonable return that the Commission authorizes BGE to have the opportunity to earn on electric distribution rate base. These charges are the way BGE recovers the prudent costs it incurs to provide safe, reliable service to customers and maintain its electric system. The EmPOWER Maryland Charge in the Delivery section of the bill as well as the line items in the Taxes and Fees section of the bill are not set during base rate proceedings and are not impacted by this filing.[^15]

**Q. Can you expand on why investments are required on BGE's electric system?**

A. BGE is focused on ensuring that the investments it makes in the electric distribution system provide tangible benefits to our customers via safe and reliable service. The Company's reliability metrics prove these investments have driven value and benefits for customers. In 2024, BGE had the best electric service reliability in the Company's history, which was a direct result of the investments that are being recovered through distribution rates. As previously noted, in its current and near-term workplans, in the interest of balancing affordability, BGE is not prioritizing improving reliability or resilience and anticipates that reliability metrics may at best remain constant and may even begin declining in the future. Additionally, electrification programs are going to necessitate the need for continued investment in BGE's electric systems. More recently, in May 2026, unseasonably hot temperatures, coupled with PJM transmission and generation outages for maintenance and system upgrades, drove much higher demand than normal on the electric system, requiring close monitoring and the specter of possible load shedding. BGE's dedicated employees worked throughout the heat event to ensure the system safely and successfully managed the higher load despite challenging system configurations. This is a tangible, clear reminder that maintaining the safety and reliability of the electric system is of paramount importance to Maryland, and to BGE's customers.

**Q. Mr. Frain, how has BGE supported its customers during this period of rising energy costs?**

A. As discussed earlier in my testimony, many customers experienced higher electric bills in 2025 compared to prior years due to factors largely outside of BGE's control, including increased electric supply prices driven by energy market conditions and the impact of extreme weather on customer usage. BGE offers multiple programs to assist customers facing financial challenges, with some of the most utilized options being Payment Arrangements, Due Date Extensions, and Budget Billing. Payment Arrangements allow customers to spread outstanding balances over time, reducing immediate financial strain. Due Date Extensions provide short-term flexibility to avoid late fees and service interruptions. Budget Billing helps customers manage seasonal fluctuations by averaging energy costs into predictable monthly payments. All of these options are easily accessible online and through BGE's Customer Care Center, ensuring customers can choose the channel that best meets their needs.

BGE also partners closely with the Fuel Fund of Maryland (the "Fuel Fund") to support customer affordability. The Fuel Fund is a donation-based, nonprofit organization dedicated to helping Maryland residents who are facing home utility hardships, regardless of their income qualifications. BGE partners closely with the Fuel Fund to extend help beyond state programs, such as the facilitation of donations through initiatives like the Dollar Donation Fund. BGE also continues to provide Commission-approved matching bill credits for Fuel Fund recipients. This partnership between BGE and Fuel Fund serves to deliver meaningful financial assistance to households beyond the reach of income-qualified programs administered through the Office of Home Energy Programs ("OHEP").

**Q. How is BGE engaging with customers about rising energy costs and assistance options?**

A. Through the various programs mentioned above, BGE has increased its efforts to help low- and moderate-income customers who are struggling with the financial impacts of rising energy costs. However, BGE knows most of these financial assistance programs are only temporary measures which, while providing necessary immediate relief to many customers, are not long-term solutions. Through regular outreach events and open houses in the various communities we serve, the Company seeks to improve customers' understanding of their bills and promotes available income-qualified energy assistance programs. In addition, BGE also promotes energy efficiency programs to empower customers to reduce energy usage and lower costs. These efforts not only support affordability but also empower customers to make informed decisions about their energy consumption, reinforcing BGE's commitment to delivering customer-focused solutions.

**Q. Are there any additional efforts you would like to mention?**

A. Yes. As part of this filing, BGE is proposing to offer a new, voluntary prepaid service program called BGE FlexPay as an additional tool that would allow customers to exercise greater control over their energy bills, allowing customers to determine what amount of energy is affordable to them. Under this program, customers can choose to prepay for their anticipated energy consumption and, through regular communication from BGE and self-service digital tools, can track how their usage is trending in relation to their account balance. I will discuss this program in more detail in Section VIII of my testimony.

**Q. Are there any rate mechanisms being implemented soon which would specifically address rising energy bills of Maryland's limited-income customers?**

A. Yes. BGE, as part of a stakeholder process, developed and proposed a Limited Income Mechanism ("LIM") to benefit eligible limited-income utility customers in Maryland as part of the Commission-initiated PC59 Work Group on Energy Burdens ("PC59 Work Group"), which was initiated in response to PUA § 4-309(c)(1).[^16] The PC59 Work Group submitted its final report on October 1, 2025, and included, among other items, recommendations on the mechanism design, cost recovery, implementation and process, customer education, and future tasks for a Phase II of the PC59 Work Group.[^17]

Commission Order No. 92190 was issued on February 12, 2026, to address the proposed limited income mechanisms for utility customers.[^18] The Commission approved a general tiered mechanism that categorizes customers based on OHEP's poverty level identification standards as well as the customer's heating source, where customers in poverty levels 6 and below would be eligible to participate in the LIM year-round. The Commission also approved a Phase II of the PC59 Work Group to discuss several outstanding areas related to the new rate mechanism, with a specific request to focus on issues related to immediate program implementation needs first. BGE views this new rate mechanism as another affordability tool the Company will offer, which will specifically benefit the Company's lowest-income customers and provide necessary immediate relief to many customers. Given the Company is awaiting Commission approval of a limited income discount tariff, the LIM has not been incorporated into this filing.

## VII. Thriving Communities Strengthen Utility Operations

**Q. Mr. Frain, could you please explain some of the ways that BGE serves and supports its communities?**

A. Certainly. As I previously mentioned, BGE continuously engages with the communities that it serves to educate customers and address concerns regarding BGE's utility service. In addition, as an organization, BGE engages in assisting communities with other needs through employee volunteering and corporate stewardship. In 2025, BGE employees spent nearly 25,000 hours volunteering at 425 events. BGE employees actively leverage their expertise and leadership to strengthen nonprofits across the communities we serve through board service with 100 BGE employees serving as board members supporting approximately 170 nonprofit organizations. BGE also serves its community as an employer by maintaining a workforce of individuals that are representative and supportive of the community and, importantly, through BGE's workforce development efforts and local business support.

**Q. Please elaborate on workforce development and how it supports the communities BGE serves.**

A. Workforce development has long been a cornerstone of BGE's community investment strategy and its commitment to building an inclusive workforce while empowering the communities it serves. In 2025, BGE proudly celebrated ten years of workforce development impact, reflecting a journey that began with a high school internship program serving fourteen students annually and which has since grown into a multi-generational strategy that reaches more than 2,500 participants across central Maryland. Through BGE's workforce development programs, hundreds of individuals have gained access to meaningful employment and early career opportunities across the energy sector and regional economy. These efforts have resulted in 63 participants beginning full-time careers at BGE, over 180 individuals securing full-time roles with BGE contractors, and more than 335 program participants entering full-time employment with regional employer partners. In addition, more than 400 young people accessed paid summer youth employment opportunities, supporting early career exploration and workforce readiness while strengthening the long-term talent pipeline.

**Q. How else does BGE support equitable access to career opportunities within the communities BGE serves?**

A. BGE opens doors to career opportunities in our communities by removing financial barriers that can limit access to education for both students at the pre- and post-secondary education levels, as well as the adult workforce. For students, the Company has supported more than 450 scholarships and 470 persistence grants since 2020, which are critical financial gap-filling resources that help ensure students are able to continue their education, access post-secondary education, and remain on a path to completion. Complementing this financial support, BGE invests in early awareness and exposure to science, technology, engineering and mathematics through its STEM Into Action efforts, which create access to STEM programming for youth through activities such as STEM fairs, career fairs, and innovative hands-on learning experiences supported by employment engagement across the company. In collaboration with the Exelon Foundation, BGE is committed to installing 21 Cal Ripken Sr. Foundation STEM Centers in elementary schools, middle schools, and youth organizations across central Maryland between 2024 and 2026, which will provide hands-on learning environments designed to inspire and engage students in STEM and technical fields.

In terms of post-secondary education support, BGE recognizes the value and role of Historically Black Colleges and Universities in creating opportunity at the post-secondary level for more people from the communities we serve. BGE partners with nine universities across central Maryland, including Bowie State University, Coppin State University and Morgan State University, and provides for academic scholarships while prioritizing scholarship recipients for paid internships, mentorship, job shadowing and executive engagement opportunities. Since 2023, BGE has hired nine scholarship recipients into full-time positions across operational areas such as finance, environmental services, information technology, operations, and engineering.

BGE's commitment to adult workforce inclusion is further reflected in the Workforce Collaborative, which focuses on under-employed and unemployed adults seeking careers in the utility infrastructure and construction industries. Since the program launched in 2019, the Workforce Collaborative has completed 57 cohorts and graduated 485 participants, achieving an 86% job placement and 80% two-year retention rate. The program also addresses critical employment barriers by supporting 133 participants in obtaining driver's licenses and facilitating the purchase of 140 vehicles to support sustained employment.

**Q. Are there any other ways that BGE supports its community?**

A. Yes, BGE strives to hire and promote individuals who represent the communities that we serve. Our goal is to always find the best person for the job and a selection process representative of our communities makes this possible. This commitment extends further through our support of local Maryland businesses. Over the two-year period of 2024 and 2025, BGE directly spent over $916 million with businesses local to Maryland. Over that same timeframe, spend with Maryland-based businesses accounted for approximately 34% of BGE's total spend. Our commitment extends beyond what the Company spends on goods and services. BGE continues to strengthen supplier capabilities through training and development that includes focus on compliance, procurement excellence, and cybersecurity controls. These efforts ensure our partners are equipped to meet industry expectations while contributing meaningfully to the region's economic growth.

**Q. Are there any other programs or distinctions you would like to highlight regarding the Company's commitment to its community and workforce?**

A. The Company's commitment to workplace culture and values is further fostered within BGE through its support of what are known as Employee Resource Groups, or ERGs. ERGs are voluntary groups led by employees that are devoted, for example, to the interests of African-Americans, Asian-Americans, Latino-Americans, veterans, women, individuals with disabilities, the LGBTQ community, and multi-cultural and multi-national employees. Importantly, BGE's parent company, Exelon, was the first utility to sign the Obama White House's Equal Pay Pledge. Other recent recognitions and distinctions for the Exelon companies include the following:

- Named #1 on Baltimore Business Journal list of Greater Baltimore largest corporate philanthropists ranked by Local Giving, 2024, 2025;
- Maryland Legal Aid's Corporate Pro Bono Partner of the Year, 2026;
- Equal by 30 Campaign, which Exelon joined, is a public and private sector commitment to work toward equal pay, equal leadership, and equal opportunities for women in the energy sector by 2030;
- Newsweek's America's Greatest Workplaces for Black Americans 2025;
- Newsweek's America's Greatest Workplaces for Women 2024; and
- Member of the Billion Dollar Roundtable, a national organization that recognizes corporations who have achieved at least $1 billion in annual spend with minority- and women-owned businesses. Exelon was the first utility and energy services company to achieve this coveted recognition.

## VIII. BGE FlexPay Gives Customers More Flexibility and Control and Helps Make Bills More Affordable

**Q. What is the BGE FlexPay proposal?**

A. In order to provide customers with tools that allow them greater control over the affordability of their energy bills, BGE is proposing in this case to offer a new prepaid service program, what the Company is calling BGE FlexPay, in which customers can voluntarily choose to participate and which will provide them with greater control over their energy expenses by permitting advance payment for their energy use. Under this option, customers can choose to prepay for their anticipated energy consumption and, through regular communication from BGE and self-service digital tools, customers can track how their usage is trending in relation to their prepayment and add to their prepaid account as needed. Like the BGE Prepaid Pilot discussed below, the Company intends to make this voluntary program available to residential customers who use gas service and/or electric service; however, since this instant case is electric-only, the Company will seek to expand eligibility to residential gas customers in a future tariff filing.

**Q. Has the Company proposed this program before?**

A. Yes. The Prepaid Pilot was approved by the Commission in 2018 and launched in late 2019 (the "Prepaid Pilot").[^19] The Prepaid Pilot targeted enrollment of at least 1,000 residential customers, including both dual-service and electric-only customers, representing a cross-section of BGE's customer base.

**Q. How did BGE evaluate the results of the Prepaid Pilot?**

A. BGE engaged APPRISE Incorporated ("APPRISE") to conduct an independent analysis and evaluation of the Prepaid Pilot (the "APPRISE Evaluation"). APPRISE is a non-profit research institute dedicated to collecting and analyzing data and information to assess and improve energy programs, including programs that provide bill payment assistance, energy education, and energy efficiency services to low-income households; and programs that provide incentives for undertaking energy efficiency projects to residential, commercial, and industrial utility customers. BGE filed the APPRISE Evaluation with the Commission on June 1, 2021, in Case No. 9453.[^20]

**Q. What is the history of the Prepaid Pilot?**

A. Enrollment began in August 2019 when BGE sent email invitations to residential customers identified as potentially eligible for the program. During the Prepaid Pilot, well over 1,000 customers expressed interest in and submitted enrollment requests. Interested customers responded to a series of questions to confirm their eligibility and submit household income data. Initially, the enrollment process faced a slow start as only a few of the invited customers responded to the emails. Over time, however, BGE adopted new communication methods, including postcards and informational "tiles" on the BGE.com website. These additional methods, rolled out in October and November of 2019, proved more effective as 84% of participants enrolled during these months.[^21]

BGE halted enrollments on November 30, 2019, to comply with the Commission's directive to end enrollment before the onset of colder winter weather. By that time, 527 customers had enrolled in the program. Nearly 1,000 additional customers initiated enrollment at some point but, ultimately, they did not participate as they did not complete all of the enrollment steps that were required in the allowed five-day period.

BGE experienced a disenrollment rate of about 8% per month before the March 2020 start of the COVID-19 disconnection moratorium – an "attrition" rate about five percentage points lower than the average, informally reported by other utilities.[^22] With continuing attrition and no additional enrollments, there were 313 participants on November 30, 2020, the one-year anniversary of the closing of enrollment, which marked the end of the formal pilot data collection period.[^23]

**Q. What was the overall outcome of the Prepaid Pilot?**

A. The Prepaid Pilot showed the promise of a beneficial program for customers even in the face of technical and operational challenges in development, recruitment, and implementation. Prepaid service is intertwined with many core utility customer and billing functions, from meter data transmission through to payment, collections, telephone, website and mobile app functionality, and customer outbound communications. Development, testing, and launch of the service, therefore, had to be tightly coordinated with other major IT upgrades at the Company. In addition, BGE took on the challenge of offering the first prepaid program (albeit a pilot) in North America to accommodate customers in retail supplier relationships.

However, the COVID-19 pandemic prevented a full year of Prepaid Pilot experience under the conditions the Commission approved. Most importantly, with the disconnect moratorium that started in March 2020 and pandemic-era changes to customer energy usage patterns compared with the pre-pilot experience, the Prepaid Pilot was meaningfully altered and impacted.

**Q. Did BGE previously pursue any follow-up to the Prepaid Pilot program?**

A. In a June 2021 filing, BGE requested approval from the Commission to allow additional customers to enroll in the Prepaid Pilot for a new 12-month period.[^24] The Commission noted in its October 2021 order that it believed BGE's proposal for the Prepaid Pilot was a "good opportunity to learn about the positives and negatives of implementing a prepaid billing program in Maryland."[^25] However, the Commission ruled that, given the COVID-19 pandemic had not yet ended, the time was not ripe for a second pilot. The Commission concluded that "BGE may, however, refile its request at a future time, when the COVID-19 pandemic is more controlled and when health and financial circumstances have stabilized."[^26]

**Q. Please provide more details on the Prepaid Pilot's performance.**

A. As mentioned above, BGE commissioned APPRISE to conduct independent evaluation of the Prepaid Pilot employing data analysis and in-depth customer surveys. APPRISE analyzed enrollment trends, participant demographics and credit risk, energy usage, payment patterns, disconnections and program costs and benefits. Despite the COVID-induced changes noted earlier, APPRISE reported:

- All electric heating prepaid participants reduced their consumption by 1.6% compared with pre-pilot usage;
- Electric non-heating participants used 2.4% less; and gas consumption dropped 2.3%;
- For those who stayed with the program for the full year, declines were sharper: 3.9% for electric heating participants; 4.1% for electric non-heating; and 2.6% for gas usage; and
- 96% of enrolled customers were somewhat or very satisfied with the program.

**Q. What observations were made related to customer payment behavior?**

A. Prepaid Pilot participants meaningfully increased their bill payments by $111 for full-year electric heating customers and by $202 for electric non-heating customers. Compared to a control group, Prepaid Pilot customers increased their payment coverage rates by an average of 18.5 percent, while non-prepaid customers' rates declined by 4 percentage points. Prepaid customers were more successful at paying down pre-existing arrearages than those using traditional installment plans, where default rates are typically high.[^27]

**Q. What observations were made related to disconnections and how did that benefit customers participating in the Prepaid Pilot?**

A. The analysis shows that, as expected, disconnections occurred more frequently in Prepaid Pilot service, but for much shorter time periods. Among Prepaid Pilot participants, 2% were disconnected at least once the year before the pilot compared to 10% during the pre-COVID-19 pilot period, whereas the non-participant population disconnect rate was 1% during that same period.

However, as shown in Figure 2 below, the Prepaid Pilot option helped participating customers reduce the average duration of disconnections, both compared with the year prior as well as compared to non-participating customers. Prepaid Pilot customers' time without service dropped to 1 day from over 6 days in the year prior, while the control group saw the duration of disconnections increase to 4.5 days from 2.5 days. Prepay service helps customers avoid accruing new arrears, so enrolled customers typically owed very little after a disconnection – possibly up to two days' worth of service if funds were exhausted just before a weekend – which made reconnecting much less burdensome than in postpaid service.[^28] The ease of reconnection and/or disenrollment from the program made disconnection less of an impactful event for customers.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 2: Average Prepaid Pilot Disconnect Durations]

A grouped bar chart comparing the average duration of service disconnections (in days), across two groups (Prepaid Pilot participants vs. a non-participating control group) and two time periods (the year prior to the pilot vs. during the pilot). The chart conveys four data points: (1) Prepaid Pilot participants, year prior to pilot: over 6 days average disconnect duration; (2) Prepaid Pilot participants, during the pilot: 1 day average disconnect duration — a sharp decrease; (3) control group (non-participants), year prior: 2.5 days average disconnect duration; (4) control group, during the same period as the pilot: 4.5 days average disconnect duration — an increase. The chart visually demonstrates the divergence between the two groups: Prepaid Pilot participants' disconnect durations fell dramatically while the control group's disconnect durations rose over the same period, supporting the testimony's point that prepaid service reduces the practical burden of a disconnection because customers accrue little to no arrears and can restore service quickly with a small payment.

[→ See original PDF page 37 for visual rendering]

**Q. Could you please summarize the conclusions of the first Prepaid Pilot experience?**

A. APPRISE concluded that:

> Participants were likely to report benefits from participation, especially those who remain enrolled. These reported benefits include 61% who reported better control over energy expenses, 38% who reported a reduction in usage,[^29] 34% who said it helped them pay off arrearages, and 46% who reported that it helped them avoid disconnections or reduce the length of disconnections. Percentages were higher when only examining those who remained enrolled.[^30]

Several important conclusions emerged from the Prepaid Pilot and the evaluation:

1. Customers who chose to remain in the Prepaid Pilot made payments to maintain their service in amounts and frequencies that better met their needs than in post-pay service.
2. After enrolling in the Prepaid Pilot, these customers were able to reconnect service more quickly in case of disconnections compared to post-pay customers in general.
3. Customers who brought pre-existing arrearages into the Prepaid Pilot were far more successful at paying off those arrearages than customers who use BGE's traditional installment payment tools, where default rates typically reach almost 90%.
4. Prepaid Pilot customers consumed less energy, both according to the data and customers' own impressions. This is despite the fact that Prepaid Pilot customers had a higher participation in demand response programs and consumed more energy while spending more time at home during the COVID-19 pandemic.
5. None of the Prepaid Pilot participants reported adverse experiences, either proactively or in response to a post-pilot survey conducted by APPRISE.[^31]
6. Finally, customers who remained in the program were overwhelmingly satisfied with the Prepaid Pilot.

More than 75% of all BGE Prepaid Pilot participants, including those who disenrolled from the program, reported that they were satisfied with it, with more than 50% saying they were very satisfied. Further, 77% of those who remained with the Prepaid Pilot said they were very satisfied, with another 19% saying they were somewhat satisfied, for a 96% satisfaction rate among full-year program participants. Although BGE understands that prepaid service may not be a good fit for all customers, experience from the Prepaid Pilot demonstrates the benefit to customers who participated.

**Q. Why is BGE seeking approval for a new prepaid service program here?**

A. Affordability has been a focus of the Company, this Commission, and many stakeholders in recent years. After significantly colder temperatures in recent winters led to higher bills than customers have typically experienced, the time is ripe for a program such as BGE FlexPay. Thus, BGE wants to give customers the opportunity to participate in a voluntary prepaid program, whereby they are provided greater control over their energy expenses by permitting advanced payment for their energy use and providing real-time feedback to connect their energy usage behavior to its cost.

Nationally, interest in prepaid utility service is strong. In a 2025 survey, the E Source energy information group found that half of customers surveyed expressed moderate to extreme interest in prepay service. Reviewing more than 10 years of such surveys, E Source reported, "This year marks an all-time high in customer interest, with over two-thirds of respondents indicating some level of interest…"[^32]

Beyond the focus on affordability, greater customer control of energy usage is even more desirable today given the energy transformation upon which Maryland has embarked. In this environment, customers increasingly need tools that give them visibility into how much energy they are using, what it is costing them, and how their choices translate into dollars on their bill in near real time. Prepaid service delivers exactly that kind of transparency and control. Rather than receiving a single bill at the end of a monthly cycle, participating customers receive ongoing communication about their consumption trends relative to their prepaid balance, enabling them to make timely adjustments before a bill arrives.

Lastly, as mentioned previously, the COVID-19 pandemic disrupted the original conditions of the Prepaid Pilot by introducing a disconnect moratorium and significantly altering customer energy usage patterns. While the world since COVID-19 has undeniably changed, conditions following the pandemic have now stabilized, and the need for innovative offerings that can help customers manage their energy bills is only increasing, especially for those who do not benefit from income-qualified programs. A new program will now include a streamlined and stable experience.

**Q. Why is the Company proposing BGE FlexPay instead of a second Prepaid Pilot?**

A. While BGE is absolutely willing to provide all of the same reporting and evaluation that comes along with a pilot, customers simply cannot and should not have to wait for a pilot to conclude in order to begin participating in prepaid service. Given the affordability concerns customers are now expressing as well as the positive experience with the Prepaid Pilot, BGE believes it is in the best interest of customers to open prepaid service to more customers, while still retaining all of the reporting and other protective measures that come with a pilot. But we firmly believe that the customers allowed to participate in this new prepaid service program should not be capped or limited given the voluntary nature of the program, the benefits it can provide, and the current customer need and interest.

**Q. How does BGE FlexPay help residential customers manage their BGE bill?**

A. The BGE FlexPay program helps customers manage their BGE bill by fundamentally changing the way they pay for and interact with their energy service. Unlike traditional postpaid billing, where customers receive a monthly bill for energy already consumed, the BGE FlexPay program allows customers to pay for their energy usage in advance, offering greater control and transparency over their energy expenses. This optional proactive payment model empowers customers to monitor their usage in real time, make informed decisions about consumption, reducing the period of disconnected service, and avoid unexpected bills or late fees. Prepay reframes the typical monthly energy bill as a series of smaller transactions that fit within a more limited planning horizon and convert a single large purchase into a sequence of manageable purchases.

**Q. Which residential customers would be eligible to participate in BGE FlexPay?**

A. To be eligible for the BGE FlexPay prepaid service program, customers are required to have an advanced metering infrastructure (or AMI) meter, which provides the Company with daily meter reads and remote connect/disconnect capabilities. Customers who have a member of the household with a serious medical condition or need life-sustaining equipment are not eligible. Further, landlords who do not occupy the service address are not eligible.

In the Prepaid Pilot, the Commission directed that customers were only allowed to bring unpaid charges of $600 or less into the program. In this new BGE FlexPay program, BGE is requesting the Commission permit pre-program arrearage balances up to $1,000. This request is to provide customers with higher arrearages another tool to help them manage their energy bill.

In addition, the Prepaid Pilot was open to customers purchasing their electric commodity from retail suppliers. BGE initially used its existing Standard Offer Service ("SOS") rates to estimate prepaid customer bills and then developed a monthly true-up process to ensure that all customers were charged the supplier's price for the energy they consumed or the SOS price for those who chose BGE for their commodity supply. Due to the complexity and incremental costs associated with offering prepaid service to customers shopping with retail suppliers, the Company proposes the BGE FlexPay program be limited to customers on SOS.[^33] Furthermore, with the implementation of Senate Bill 1 a few years ago and the steady decline in residential enrollments with retail suppliers, limiting the BGE FlexPay program to SOS customers is both practical and cost-effective.[^34]

**Q. Are limited income customers eligible to participate in BGE FlexPay, and if so, how did the pilot experience benefit these customers?**

A. Yes. Approximately 6% of the participants in the Prepaid Pilot were recipients of OHEP grants, while 25% identified themselves as limited-income customers. This is in comparison to just under 4% of BGE's residential customer population who received OHEP grants during that time and an estimated 20% of all customer households who, according to census data, were likely eligible. Customers carrying an arrearage into the program, including those customers who are limited income, can defer payment of those amounts through a coupled payment plan. As a result, as I explained earlier, prepay service allows customers to stop accruing new arrearages while giving them the space and time to pay their existing arrearage in a manner they can better manage, all while allowing them to retain and continue using utility services.

**Q. How would the proposed BGE FlexPay program work?**

A. With approval of BGE FlexPay, the Company would conduct outreach to residential customers that meet the BGE FlexPay program criteria and invite them to participate through various methods including email, postcards, and informational banners on BGE.com. Moving to an ongoing service, rather than a time-limited pilot, also will allow BGE to better engage community stakeholders to communicate with their constituents about this service. As learned during the Prepaid Pilot, expanding to these additional channels will further increase successful enrollments. Interested customers would be directed to an online enrollment portal where they would complete a brief eligibility questionnaire, provide household income information, and review the program's terms and conditions. Once approved, the customer will be required to acknowledge and accept the terms and conditions of the program and make a $40 payment within a five-day period to process enrollment.

Customers carrying an arrearage into the program can defer payment of those amounts through a coupled payment plan. As it did during the Prepaid Pilot, BGE would apply 75 percent of each new payment to replenish the account, and 25 percent to reduce the deferred arrearage balance. The customer would have the ability to make additional payments to reduce or eliminate the deferred arrearage balance.

As the customer uses energy, the prepaid balance will be reduced accordingly. The program provides real-time access to account status, including the current balance and estimated number of days of service based on recent usage and weather forecasting. Each month, BGE will create a comprehensive statement detailing their energy usage, payments, and account activity.

BGE will send regular notifications through the customer's preferred communication channel when the balance is low and when the balance reaches zero.[^35] An auto-reload option allows customers to register for automatic payments of any selected amount ($5.00 or more), which would be triggered when their prepaid account reached a remaining balance that is enough to cover an estimated two days' worth of service. In the Prepaid Pilot, there were 95 participants who enrolled in auto-reload, with payment amounts ranging from $10 to $1,000, and an average payment of $116.26.

If the customer's prepaid balance reaches zero, service would be disconnected, typically the following business day, and in compliance with existing extreme weather prohibitions; however, a customer can restore service quickly by making a payment that brings their account balance to at least $15. Customers may also request a five-day extension, to provide extra time to make sufficient payment. The BGE FlexPay program also includes consumer protections such as no security deposits or late fees as well as the waiver of reconnection fees for participants.

The Commission previously authorized BGE to reimburse participating customers for the first payment transaction fee incurred each calendar month, which the Company proposes again as a feature of the BGE FlexPay program.[^36] During the Prepaid Pilot, participating customers could use any of BGE's payment methods or avoid transaction fees by using one of the fee-free payment options, and this would be the case with BGE FlexPay as well.

**Q. Does a customer have to commit to be in the BGE FlexPay program for a specific length of time?**

A. No, the program is completely voluntary. Customers can withdraw from the BGE FlexPay program and return to standard postpaid billing at any time, even if their service has been disconnected due to lack of prepaid funds.

**Q. Will BGE request a waiver of certain COMAR provisions to implement the BGE FlexPay program?**

A. Yes. Consistent with the Prepaid Pilot, if approved by the Commission, this program will entail interactions with customers that are not addressed by COMAR, certain Commission regulations, orders, or BGE's Electric Tariff. For this reason, BGE again seeks a waiver of certain items which are detailed in Company Exhibit JCF-2A. Company Exhibit JCF-2B is a copy of the Prepaid Pilot Terms and Conditions provided to customers, which the Company would update based on approval of this BGE FlexPay program. Company Witness Karas has included a model rider to the Electric Tariff for the BGE FlexPay program being requested herein, consistent with the language approved in the Prepaid Pilot with limited updates as requested by the Company herein.[^37]

**Q. If approved, when would the Company launch the BGE FlexPay program?**

A. If the Commission approves the BGE FlexPay program, the Company anticipates launching within 17 to 20 months following the Commission's decision. This timeline allows for necessary system testing, program enhancements, and customer outreach to ensure successful implementation. Enrollment would begin after these preparatory steps.

**Q. What costs will be incurred to establish the BGE FlexPay program?**

A. Costs to transition to the BGE FlexPay program would include costs for the annual licensing fees to the prepaid vendor and IT enhancements to validate the system functionality as well as improve the customer experience. BGE estimates the electric distribution share of the initial project costs will total approximately $3.4 million of capital expenditures and $1.1 million of O&M expenses.[^38] Additional costs could come from unexpected program revisions or the discovery of defects in the existing BGE billing system codes following the billing system upgrades. This prepaid functionality has not been used since 2021 and will need to be tested to determine any revisions that may be needed. In addition, ongoing program management costs will be incurred, including vendor licensing fees and system support. See Part II of my testimony for the associated ratemaking adjustment to include the costs to implement BGE FlexPay in the revenue requirement.[^39]

## IX. A Rider Is Necessary for BGE to Meet Current Customer Storm Restoration Expectations

**Q. How does the Company currently recover storm restoration expenses?**

A. How storm restoration expenses have traditionally been recovered differs depending on the type of event. Prior to Case No. 9645 – the Company's first MYP proceeding – BGE was allowed to recover the five-year average of major outage event (as defined by COMAR 20.50.01.03.B.32) restoration expenses.[^40] In Order No. 89678 in Case No. 9645, the Commission granted BGE's proposal that a regulatory asset be utilized to track incremental major outage event restoration expenses. However, as BGE did not forecast major outage event restoration expense due to the volatility, no major outage event restoration expense was reflected in the base rates approved by the Commission in Case Nos. 9645 and 9692 and all incremental major outage event restoration expenses were deferred to the regulatory asset and recovery sought in the next base rate case.

For non-major outage events, however, base rates include a five-year average of non-major incremental storm restoration expenses.

**Q. What is the Company proposing in this filing?**

A. As noted earlier in my testimony, the Company proposes to implement a Storm Restoration Expense Rider (the "Storm Rider") for the differences between the level of incremental storm restoration expenses actually incurred and incremental storm restoration expenses authorized as the baseline in rates – regardless of the type of the storm event. This proposal aligns the cost recovery methodology going forward for major and non-major storm event incremental expenses.

**Q. Why is the Company proposing this Storm Rider?**

A. This framework is designed to align customer rates more closely with actual storm-related expenses while mitigating volatility in customer bills from year to year. Historically, storm restoration response has been one of the most unpredictable and most significant factors impacting BGE's maintenance and operation of the electric distribution system. Due to the uncertainty surrounding the ratemaking framework in Maryland, in order to be able to continue effective response to storm events and to maintain the storm outage restoration experience to which customers have become accustomed, the Company needs a mechanism for timely funding and recovery of storm restoration expenses.

As supported in the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Wright, the impact storms have on the electric distribution system is volatile and unpredictable, and, while the impact of and number of outages customers experience due to storms has lessened over recent years as a result of investments in distribution automation technology, storms continue to significantly damage utility equipment.[^41]

**Q. Specifically, what type of costs are you proposing be eligible for recovery through the Storm Rider mechanism?**

A. The scope of costs eligible for recovery through the Storm Rider is limited to incremental O&M expenses associated with storm restoration for both major and non-major storm events and excludes capital expenditures. Incremental O&M expenses for storm restoration are expenses to prepare for and remediate storm damage to the distribution system, including, but not limited to, overtime wages for Company employees, cost of outside service providers and mutual assistance, and materials and supplies. In contrast, non-incremental O&M expenses—defined as straight-time wages and benefits for Company employees performing storm restoration activities—would be excluded from the rider and will continue to be recovered solely through base rates. This distinction ensures that only those costs that vary with storm activity are subject to rider treatment, maintaining cost discipline and consistency with ratemaking principles.

**Q. Please explain how the Storm Rider would operate.**

A. Under the proposed Storm Rider, the difference, or imbalance, between actual incremental storm restoration O&M expense for a 12-month period and the baseline level of incremental storm O&M expense embedded in base rates would be eligible for recovery or refund through the rider mechanism. The differences would be tracked through a regulatory asset or liability, ensuring appropriate accounting treatment and transparency over time.

The baseline level of storm restoration expense reflected in proposed rates is a normalized level of incremental storm O&M expense instead of historic test year actual expense. As discussed further in Part II of my testimony, I am proposing adjustments to normalize incremental storm restoration O&M expense in the historic test year to a weighted five-year average of actual incremental storm O&M expenses. This approach provides a reasonable and representative estimate of expected storm-related expenses over time, smoothing the effects of variability in annual storm activity. The use of a weighted five-year average as the baseline ensures that base rates reflect typical storm conditions while the Storm Rider addresses deviations from that baseline. See the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Wright for information on the recent storm trends supporting the use of a weighted average.[^42]

The imbalance amount to be recovered or refunded through the Storm Rider would be amortized over a three-year period and subject to the application of interest using the Company's most recent authorized rate of return. This extended recovery time period is designed to enhance rate stability for customers. Absent such a mechanism, storm-related expenses could cause more significant fluctuations in rates from year to year, particularly following periods of unusually high storm activity. By spreading the recovery or refund of incremental storm expenses over the proposed three-year recovery period, the rider moderates the rate impacts associated with these events and promotes greater predictability for customers.

In addition, the annual rate filing will include a true-up component to account for any over- or under-collection of prior year Storm Rider amounts, with the application of interest on the over- or under-collection using the Company's most recent authorized rate of return. This ensures that customers are protected and neither overpay nor underpay for incremental storm expenses over time. Each annual Storm Rider filing will also provide an opportunity for regulatory review of the prudency of the incremental storm O&M expense incurred by the Company, thereby maintaining appropriate oversight and accountability. Company Witness Karas has included the proposed Storm Rider in her Direct Testimony.[^43]

**Q. Mr. Frain, how and when does the Company propose to begin operating under the proposed Storm Rider mechanism?**

A. Upon Commission approval of the proposed Storm Rider mechanism, the Company would begin tracking the difference between actual incremental storm O&M expense and the baseline incremental storm O&M expense for the remainder of the 2027 calendar year.[^44] The first rate filing, including prudency review support for incremental storm O&M expenses, would be filed by the end of May 2028 proposing a charge or refund rate effective for July 2028 – June 2029 billings. Future year filings will use actual storm restoration expense for a calendar year and set the Storm Rider rate to change annually with July billings.

**Q. Can you walk me through an example of how this would work in practice?**

A. Yes, I can. See Figure 3 below for an illustrative example of a hypothetical year operating under this Storm Rider mechanism. This example assumes that $100 is the authorized incremental storm expense included in base rates.
