In a given year, the Company would compare the incremental storm restoration expenses included in electric distribution rates with the actual incremental storm restoration expenses incurred by the Company for the twelve-month period under review. If the Company actually incurred $75 for the twelve-month period, then the difference, or imbalance, of $25 would be deferred into a regulatory liability as it represents an amount owed to customers. In the annual rider filing, that imbalance, adjusted for interest, would be given back to customers over a three-year period. However, if the Company actually incurred $125 in that twelve-month period instead of $75, then the difference of $25 would be deferred into a regulatory asset as it represents an additional amount to be charged to customers, and recovery of that amount, adjusted for interest, from customers would be spread across a three-year period.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 3: Illustrative Storm Rider Example]

A schematic/illustrative diagram walking through two hypothetical scenarios of the Storm Rider mechanism, both starting from the same baseline: $100 of incremental storm restoration expense is authorized in base rates for the year. Scenario A (under-spend): actual incremental storm restoration expense incurred is $75, which is $25 less than the $100 baseline; this $25 difference is recorded as a regulatory liability (an amount BGE owes customers) and is refunded to customers, with interest, over the following three-year period. Scenario B (over-spend): actual incremental storm restoration expense incurred is $125, which is $25 more than the $100 baseline; this $25 difference is recorded as a regulatory asset (an amount customers owe BGE) and is recovered from customers, with interest, over the following three-year period. The figure is meant to illustrate the symmetric, cost-based nature of the rider: whether BGE spends more or less than the baseline, only the actual difference (not the full storm expense) flows through the rider, and it flows in the direction that keeps customers whole (a refund when BGE spends less, a surcharge when BGE spends more), smoothed over three years.

[→ See original PDF page 53 for visual rendering]

**Q. How do customers benefit from the Storm Rider proposal?**

A. This approach provides clear customer benefit by ensuring that customers ultimately pay no more or no less than actual incremental storm event costs incurred by the Company, rather than relying on the historic test year amounts that do not reflect actual storm restoration conditions in the rate effective period. Further, by spreading the recovery of incremental storm expenses over the proposed three-year recovery period, the mechanism moderates the rate impacts associated with these events and promotes greater rate predictability for customers while balancing that predictability with the actual costs of storm restoration.

Further, as I previously stated, due to the uncertainty surrounding the ratemaking framework in Maryland, the Company needs a mechanism for more timely funding of storm restoration costs to be able to continue effectively responding to storm events and to maintain the storm outage restoration experience to which customers have become accustomed.

Overall, the proposed Storm Rider represents a balanced and transparent framework that appropriately allocates storm-related cost risk between the Company and its customers. It ensures that customers pay for actual, prudently incurred incremental storm restoration expenses while providing rate stability and maintaining regulatory oversight through the annual Storm Rider filing.

## X. The Baltimore City Conduit Agreement Overwhelmingly Benefits Customers

**Q. Please summarize the purpose of this section of your testimony.**

A. The purpose of this section of my testimony is to:

1. present the overwhelming benefits of the 2023 Conduit Agreement between BGE and Baltimore City concerning BGE's installation of its facilities within the City's conduit system (the "2023 Conduit Agreement");
2. report the results of the BCA that the Commission directed the Company to perform for the 2023 Conduit Agreement;[^45] and
3. support the ratemaking adjustments necessary to recover the conduit regulatory asset permitted by the Commission in Order No. 92106, related to the 2023 Final Reconciliation.[^46]

My testimony, including the BCA results performed by independent consultant West Monroe Partners LLC ("West Monroe"), supports the Company's assertion that the 2023 Conduit Agreement is a win-win outcome for customers and the Company.

**Q. Before going further, can you please provide a brief overview of Baltimore City's conduit system and BGE's use of the system?**

A. Yes. Baltimore City owns and operates the underground conduit system that traverses the City, and BGE and other tenants occupy space in that system. In fact, BGE facilities in Baltimore City are required to be placed within the City conduit system. BGE is the largest tenant by far in the conduit system, occupying over three quarters of the system. BGE and the other occupants are required to pay the City for maintenance of the conduit system.

Since at least 1903, BGE and Baltimore City have had agreements with respect to the Baltimore City conduit system. However, unlike other jurisdictions where rate-regulated public utilities own their conduit system, Baltimore City has ownership of the entire conduit system in its jurisdiction. As a consequence, BGE is required to periodically renew its agreement with Baltimore City regarding the fees BGE is to pay for maintenance of the conduit system, and the City has sought to increase those fees upon each such renewal. There is no dispute that the Company must pay fees to the City in connection with the Company's pro rata maintenance of the conduit system.

**Q. Please describe the circumstances leading up to BGE and Baltimore City entering into the 2023 Conduit Agreement.**

A. Prior to 2016, Baltimore City essentially used a "run to failure" maintenance approach for the conduit system. In other words, almost all repairs were reactive in nature and related to collapsed or obstructed segments of conduit or manhole integrity issues. Beginning around 2016, the City began to proactively replace entire spans of outdated conduit and associated manholes. As a result, Baltimore City significantly increased the rates of all users of its conduit system to recover the significant increase in their costs.

In 2021, the then-existing conduit agreement between BGE and Baltimore City expired, and the two parties commenced negotiations of a new agreement. As testified to by BGE's then chief financial officer in Case No. 9692, the two parties negotiated for a year and a half before coming to an impasse.[^47] Baltimore City insisted on a new agreement that spanned a seven-year time period as the 2023 Conduit Agreement could with extensions, but included payments from BGE that were "north of $300 million."[^48] BGE deemed the City's proposal to be unworkable, given the rate impact to customers, and developed an alternative approach that resulted in the 2023 Conduit Agreement.

**Q. Can you please summarize the terms of the 2023 Conduit Agreement?**

A. Under the 2023 Conduit Agreement, which has an initial term from 2023 – 2026,[^49] BGE secured the exclusive right to identify, select, and execute improvements to the City's conduit system. These rights expressly allow BGE to determine which upgrades to pursue so that each investment directly benefits its electric distribution system and, by extension, its electric customers. As a result of BGE's perpetual rights to the conduit system, qualifying improvements made by BGE may be capitalized on BGE's books. Over the initial term of the 2023 Conduit Agreement, BGE is required to invest a total of $120 million in capital improvements.

In addition, BGE agreed to pay the City $14 million in 2023 to cover the interim period prior to execution of the agreement, and to make ongoing annual payments of $1.5 million representing its pro rata share of the maintenance costs associated with the conduit system. This renegotiated structure represents a significant reduction in occupancy fees relative to the City's prior proposal, which would have required BGE to pay no less than $300 million over a seven-year period.

**Q. What are the ratemaking impacts associated with BGE's ability to capitalize improvements to the conduit system under the 2023 Conduit Agreement?**

A. Under accounting rules governing the 2023 Agreement, BGE is able to capitalize as leasehold improvements the investments it makes in the conduit system.[^50] Once placed in service, each asset will be depreciated ratably over the asset's long estimated useful life. This has the effect of spreading cost recovery over the approximate 50-year lifetime of the improvements. The resulting annual revenue requirements are significantly less over the term of the 2023 Conduit Agreement than they would have been under the City's proposal. Consistent with prior agreements, the City's latest proposal would have resulted in dollar-for-dollar recovery of the annual lease payments.

**Q. Please summarize why the Company entered into the 2023 Conduit Agreement.**

A. The Company entered into the 2023 Conduit Agreement as it is clearly in the interests of our customers. First and foremost, the 2023 Conduit Agreement results in significant reductions to our customers' bills, as explained above. Second, the 2023 Conduit Agreement provides BGE with the exclusive right to select and execute infrastructure investments to the conduit system that are most beneficial to BGE's electric distribution system and therefore most beneficial to BGE customers. Third, the 2023 Conduit Agreement results in a significant reduction in the annual amount of lease expense payments made to Baltimore City, which would otherwise be recovered dollar-for-dollar every year from our customers. Fourth, the 2023 Conduit Agreement results in cost certainty as well as value delivered to BGE customers over the agreement's term by locking in the amount of occupancy fees and required levels of capital improvements. Finally, as it has since at least 1903, the Company was required to enter into an agreement with Baltimore City, who owns the conduit system.

**Q. Is a BCA required to justify recovery of the 2023 Conduit work?**

A. No. While the Company has complied fully with the Commission's directive to prepare a BCA, ratemaking does not require recovery of infrastructure investments to be conditioned on the performance of a BCA. The controlling question is whether the expenditures were prudently incurred and whether the resulting electric facilities placed in service are used and useful in providing regulated service, not whether they are accompanied by a particular analytical study. Nothing in the 2023 Conduit Agreement alters those fundamental principles. In other words, the question is not whether BGE or the City of Baltimore should have negotiated a different agreement, but whether the work produced pursuant to the agreement that has been executed is prudently incurred and whether the improvements are used and useful to providing electric service.

BGE is obligated to provide safe and reliable electric distribution service throughout its service territory, including Baltimore City. In Baltimore City, BGE is required to place its facilities in the City-owned conduit system. Because BGE has no real alternative to using the City-owned conduit system, investments to rehabilitate and modernize that system are not discretionary. Rather, they are fundamental prerequisites to fulfilling the Company's obligation to provide safe and reliable service. Accordingly, there can be no serious dispute that investments made pursuant to the 2023 Agreement are prudently incurred and used and useful, regardless of a BCA.

**Q. Why don't the improvements made pursuant to the 2023 Agreement system warrant a BCA?**

A. The Baltimore City conduit system is not an optional modernization program or an elective investment opportunity. It is the legally mandated platform through which BGE must operate its underground electric system within the City of Baltimore in order to provide electric service in the area. For more than a century, municipal ordinances have required utilities to locate their facilities in the City-owned conduit network and to pay for that privilege. BGE therefore has no lawful or practical alternative to utilizing the conduit system to serve its Baltimore City customers. As a result, the conduit performs the same essential function in Baltimore City that poles, duct banks, conduits, and rights-of-way perform elsewhere in the Company's service territory—it is core delivery infrastructure without which customers cannot receive electric service. Investments associated with the conduit system are therefore not discretionary; they are necessary to comply with legal obligations and to maintain the basic operability of the distribution system. Because the Baltimore City conduit work is indispensable to the provision of electric service in the Baltimore area, investments to prevent collapse, mitigate safety hazards, and reduce outage risk cannot reasonably be considered imprudent or not used and useful. Rather, they are required to ensure the continued provision of safe and reliable service and preserve the basic operability of the electric system. Subjecting such mandatory, foundational infrastructure to an ex ante BCA as a condition of recovery would invert traditional utility regulation by requiring a utility to economically justify continued compliance with its legal duty to serve.

**Q. Apart from any BCA calculation, how is the 2023 Conduit Agreement more customer-beneficial than prior conduit agreements previously deemed prudent by the Commission?**

A. The 2023 Conduit Agreement represents a material improvement for BGE and its customers over prior arrangements that the Commission previously deemed prudent. Under those earlier agreements, BGE was required to remit substantial conduit fees to Baltimore City, after which the City retained discretion over how those funds were spent. Those expenditures could include salaries, administrative overhead, and generalized municipal conduit activities that were not necessarily targeted to protecting BGE's electric facilities or reducing outage risk for BGE customers. Although those earlier agreements were deemed prudent for ratemaking purposes, they left BGE with little control over whether the Company's payments translated into timely or strategically located infrastructure improvements that benefited BGE customers. The 2023 Conduit Agreement fundamentally alters that dynamic in a manner that is substantially more protective of customers. Rather than making largely unrestricted payments to the City, BGE now directly performs and funds capital improvements that it selects based on engineering judgment and system need. The Company determines which conduit segments to replace, which manholes to rebuild, and which projects will most effectively protect its electric systems and enhance reliability for customers. This ensures that every dollar invested is tied to tangible physical improvements to infrastructure that supports electric service for our customers, rather than being absorbed into broader municipal programs over which the Company has no control.

By locking in cost certainty, directing investments toward high-value system upgrades, and eliminating the risk that customer funds will be diverted to non-electric purposes, the 2023 Conduit Agreement is affirmatively and objectively more customer-beneficial than the agreements previously found acceptable by the Commission. The replacement of failing terracotta and Orangeburg conduit reduces the risk of cable damage, water intrusion, fires, and structural collapse—events that impose significant costs on customers through service interruptions, emergency restoration expenses, traffic disruptions, and public safety hazards. These well-publicized events have occurred repeatedly in the conduit system and will continue absent modernization consistent with the rest of BGE's service territory. These operational benefits are neither speculative nor abstract; like other parts of BGE's service territory, they translate directly into fewer outages, faster restoration times, enhanced public safety, and a more stable distribution system serving one of the most densely populated areas of BGE's service territory. Accordingly, these customer benefits are concrete and objectively beneficial without reliance on a formal BCA.

**Q. Please discuss the BCA that was performed.**

A. As mentioned earlier, the Company engaged an independent consultant, West Monroe, to develop a BCA consistent with the recommendations and parameters of Staff Witness Dererie in Case No. 9692. The final report is included as Company Exhibit JCF-3. The supporting model will be provided in Microsoft Excel format to parties as part of the Company's release of voluntary supporting workpapers. The primary BCA test resulted in a BCA ratio of 1.15, reflecting that benefits exceed costs.[^51] Two secondary tests (the Utility Cost Test ("UCT") and Rate Impact Measure ("RIM") tests) were also performed with positive results (1.35 and 1.0, respectively), further supporting BGE's assertion that the 2023 Agreement is a win-win outcome for customers and the Company under these recognized BCA models.

**Q. Please provide an overview of the BCA report.**

A. The report identifies the long-term benefits and costs of the 2023 Conduit Agreement to carry out essential capital improvements to the City's underground conduit system. The report describes the 2023 Conduit Agreement's features and its benefits in comparison to the costs. It is based on a detailed review of the City's conduit work and BGE's activities to date, and it explains how the work is carried out in important new ways under the 2023 Agreement.

**Q. How was the primary BCA ratio derived?**

A. The BCA ratio of 1.15 was calculated using the RIM test with quantifiable reliability benefits included, as described in further detail in the report. The math used to derive the BCA test results is shown below.

**Equation:**
$$\text{BCA Ratio} = \frac{\text{Avoided Costs} + \text{Incremental Reliability Benefits}}{\text{Costs}}$$

The analysis reflects approximately $235 million in total costs, offset by roughly $319 million in avoided costs and additional outage-related benefits quantified using the U.S. Department of Energy's Interruption Cost Estimate ("ICE") model. All values were developed on a revenue requirement basis and discounted to present value, resulting in a benefit-cost ratio greater than 1.0.

**Q. Did West Monroe consider any other benefits to include in the BCA?**

A. Yes. West Monroe identified several qualitative benefits, as noted on page 32 of the report and listed below:

- Lower long-term lease and capital costs due to lower repairs by building to higher standards;
- Improved safety due to building with higher standards;
- Improved economic activity by serving new load requirements, and new services like fiber, more quickly;
- Higher levels of electrical system resiliency, which means fewer economic losses that are caused by outages and failures; and
- Reduced greenhouse gas emissions due to fewer truck trips, reduced traffic disruptions and faster conduction of work.

These additional benefits are difficult to quantify and were excluded from the calculation used to develop the BCA Ratio of 1.15. This exclusion should not diminish the importance of those benefits when the Commission ultimately decides whether or not benefits exceed costs under the 2023 Conduit Agreement.

**Q. Are there other items for which you would like to make the Commission aware regarding the development of the BCA?**

A. Yes. In accordance with the Commission's directive in Order No. 91659 in Case No. 9692, BGE met with OPC and Staff ahead of this filing. As noted in its October 31, 2025 filing with the Commission, BGE held two meetings with Staff and OPC since the issuance of Order No. 91659 where the Company described the BCA being conducted, including the numerous inputs to the analysis, and responded to questions from both Staff and OPC.[^52] The outcome of the unified BCA workgroup was included as part of the discussion. In developing the BCA, the Company considered the feedback provided by Staff and OPC as a result of these meetings.

**Q. You mentioned that the Conduit Agreement with Baltimore City is periodically renewed. Could you please provide an update on its status?**

A. Yes. BGE is currently in active negotiations with Baltimore City regarding the conduit agreement. The City has informed BGE that it does not intend to extend the current agreement's initial four-year term (2023–2026) beyond December 31, 2026. Notwithstanding that position, the parties are engaged in ongoing discussions to establish terms for a successor agreement that will be mutually beneficial. While BGE is negotiating in good faith with the City, the Company's primary objective is to protect customer affordability by seeking to keep any new costs as close to flat as possible. As noted previously, each periodic renewal of the conduit agreement has included negotiations over the amount of fees BGE pays for maintenance of the conduit system, and the City has historically sought increases in those fees. To the extent the new agreement results in incremental capital investment requirements or increased O&M expenses due to higher fees payable to the City, I am requesting regulatory asset treatment for both the depreciation associated with any incremental capital investments and any additional O&M expense. In the event that any costs are incurred and deferred into the proposed conduit fee regulatory asset reflected in the adjustments presented in Part II of my testimony during the course of this proceeding and before the hearings, the Company will seek recovery in this proceeding.[^53] If such incremental costs are incurred and deferred into the proposed conduit fee regulatory asset subsequent to the hearings in this proceeding, the Company will seek recovery of those amounts in a future base rate case proceeding.
