## Part II – Revenue Requirement Testimony

**Q. What is the purpose of Part II of your testimony in this proceeding?**

A. Part II of my Direct Testimony is offered in support of BGE's requested electric revenue adjustment. My Direct Testimony, along with exhibits, also provides a review of historic capital additions and test year O&M expenses incurred to provide safe and reliable service to customers. Additionally, my Direct Testimony, along with the Direct Testimonies of Company Witnesses Wright and Cloyd, demonstrates the prudency of certain electric distribution capital projects related to Order No. 92106 related to the 2023 Final Reconciliation in Case No. 9645.[^54]

**Q. Are you sponsoring any exhibits in Part II of your Direct Testimony?**

A. Yes. I have prepared the following exhibits:

- Company Exhibit JCF-4 – Electric Distribution Revenue Requirement Summary
- Company Exhibit JCF-5 – Electric Distribution Rate Base and Operating Income Summary
- Company Exhibit JCF-6 – Electric Distribution Revenue Requirement (Unadjusted and Adjusted)
- Company Exhibit JCF-7 – Electric Distribution Plant Additions - Summary by Category and Project
- Company Exhibit JCF-8 – Electric Distribution Operations and Maintenance Expenses - Summary by FERC Account

**Q. How is Part II of your testimony organized?**

A. Part II of my Direct Testimony is organized as follows:

- First, I will set forth BGE's revenue requirement deficiency and requested revenue adjustment.
- Second, I will discuss certain tax developments including the Net Operating Loss Carryforward ("NOLC") and the Corporate Alternative Minimum Tax ("CAMT").
- Third, I will walk through my revenue requirement exhibits including Company Exhibits JCF-4 through JCF-6, which support the $156.1 million revenue requirement the Company is requesting in this proceeding.
- Fourth, I will review the historic capital additions and test year O&M expenses incurred to provide safe and reliable service to customers, including walking through Company Exhibit JCF-7, which is co-sponsored by Company Witnesses Cloyd and Wright, and Company Exhibit JCF-8.

## XI. Overview of BGE's Request

**Q. Please summarize BGE's request for an adjustment to its electric revenue requirement in this proceeding?**

A. As noted earlier in my testimony and as set forth below, BGE's present electric distribution base rates are insufficient to provide the Company a reasonable opportunity to earn the 7.68% rate of return which is supported by the Company in this proceeding. As a result of the passage of the Utility RELIEF (Reducing Energy Load Inflation for Everyday Families) Act in 2026[^55] and, specifically, its temporary prohibition on the use of forecasted test years,[^56] the Company's demonstrated need is based on a traditional historic test year reflecting operating results for the twelve months ended March 31, 2026 (i.e., the test year), adjusted for known and measurable changes in expenses and components of rate base. I am also sponsoring the development of the revenue requirement, including various pro forma adjustments, to support the Company's request for an adjustment in electric base distribution rates of $156.1 million.

As supported in the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Karas, the requested rate adjustment would result in an overall increase of 2.8 percent in total electric bills if granted in full, but the impact will vary from rate schedule to rate schedule and from customer to customer.[^57] The total monthly bill for a residential electric customer with average monthly usage of 851 kWh is expected to increase by approximately 4 percent (or about $8 per month).[^58] With the proposed increase, the electric distribution cost (delivery cost) would comprise approximately 30 percent (approximately $60) of the total electric bill.

As I noted in Part I of my testimony above, the Company needs to continue investing in the electric distribution system in order to maintain a safe and dependable electric system, as well as fulfill the minimum regulatory and statutory requirements. The Company is concentrating on maintenance and meeting essential obligations, and in the interest of affordability is not proposing to specifically enhance or proactively improve system resiliency or reliability.

## XII. Tax Developments

**Q. Are there any particular topics that you want to discuss with respect to your testimony and exhibits at the outset?**

A. Yes. I want to first address certain tax items that impact the revenue requirement deficiency presented in my exhibits. The purpose of this section of my Direct Testimony is to explain: 1) Net Operating Loss Carryforward ("NOLC") adjustments made to the income tax regulatory liability and accumulated deferred income taxes included in rate base to comply with the "normalization" provisions under federal tax law; and 2) the effect of new guidance impacting the determination of the Corporate Alternative Minimum tax ("CAMT") and related ratemaking adjustments in determining income taxes for ratemaking purposes.

### A. Ratemaking Adjustments Related to the Treatment of Federal Net Operating Loss Carryforwards

**Q. What is the ratemaking treatment for accumulated deferred income taxes related to tax net operating loss carryforwards ("NOLC")?**

A. On June 28, 2024, the Internal Revenue Service ("IRS") published a series of private letter rulings ("PLRs") requested by another taxpayer that provide guidance with respect to the application of the "normalization" rules and the proper treatment of income tax benefits associated with tax net operating losses among affiliates within a consolidated group for ratemaking purposes.[^59] Although a PLR can only be relied on by the taxpayer that received it, it is instructive to other taxpayers as to the IRS's point of view on a particular subject.

For ratemaking purposes, to comply with the "normalization" rules, BGE corrected its historic methodology for NOLCs to follow the PLRs.

**Q. Did BGE seek its own PLR from the IRS to confirm the proper application of the "normalization" rules with respect to NOLC?**

A. Yes. BGE submitted its own PLR request with the IRS on March 27, 2025, to confirm the proper application of the "normalization" rules with respect to NOLC.

**Q. Has the IRS responded to BGE's PLR request?**

A. No. As of the date of this testimony, BGE has not received a response from the IRS.

**Q. When does BGE expect to receive a response from the IRS?**

A. There is no statutory deadline for a response from the IRS. At this point, given the delays, it is difficult to estimate the exact timing for a response. It is my understanding that it is reasonable to assume the IRS will respond within the next 12 months.

**Q. What are the IRS's "normalization" rules?**

A. The "normalization" rules determine the period of time that regulated utilities are permitted to share the benefit of accelerated tax depreciation with customers. Under "normalization," a utility receives the tax benefit of accelerated tax depreciation in the early years of an asset's regulatory useful life and passes that benefit through to customers ratably over the regulatory useful life of the asset in the form of reduced rates. The regulated utility shares the tax benefit of accelerated tax depreciation with customers by including the related deferred income taxes as a reduction to rate base.

**Q. What is a "normalization" violation?**

A. A "normalization" violation occurs when a regulated utility uses ratemaking or accounting methods that are inconsistent with the IRS "normalization" rules.

**Q. What are the consequences of a "normalization" violation?**

A. A "normalization" violation results in the loss of the right to claim accelerated tax depreciation on assets in service as of the date of the violation and any future asset additions. The inability to claim deductions for accelerated tax depreciation would result in higher cash taxes and eliminate a cost-free source of capital to the company, which results in higher costs that are shared with customers. Additionally, a dollar-for-dollar penalty would also be assessed related to excess deferred income taxes that are flowed back to customers.

**Q. What is a tax net operating loss?**

A. A tax net operating loss occurs when a company's allowable tax deductions exceed its gross income for a tax year. For federal income tax purposes, in certain tax years, a corporate taxpayer is permitted to either carry back or carry forward those losses to reduce taxable income in prior or future tax years. However, if a corporation is a member of a consolidated group, its tax net operating losses are required to reduce the taxable income of other entities within that group.

**Q. What was BGE's historic methodology for calculating the allocation of income taxes?**

A. Exelon and its affiliated subsidiaries, including BGE, file a consolidated federal income tax return. As the common parent of an affiliated group of subsidiaries, Exelon's consolidated federal income tax liability is determined based on each subsidiary's separate tax return liability.

Under this methodology and the IRS's consolidated tax return rules, BGE calculates and pays taxes based on its separate corporate books and records. BGE is paid by another Exelon subsidiary for any tax losses or other tax attributes (e.g., tax credits) to the extent the tax losses or tax attributes are utilized in Exelon's consolidated return to offset the tax liability of that subsidiary.

By operation of the consolidated return rules, BGE's federal tax losses and tax credits are utilized sooner than they otherwise would be because of taxes attributable to other members of the Exelon consolidated group.[^60]

**Q. Can you provide an illustrative example of BGE's historic methodology?**

A. Yes. As an illustrative example, if BGE generates a separate company taxable loss (due to accelerated tax depreciation) of $100 and another entity (Entity B) within Exelon's consolidated group reports taxable income of $150, Exelon would report $50 of taxable income on its consolidated tax return. Pursuant to the consolidated tax return rules, Exelon's tax sharing agreement, and separate company accounting, Entity B would pay its separate company tax liability associated with the $150 in taxable income to Exelon. Exelon would use that payment from Entity B to pay the IRS and would reimburse BGE for the tax benefit associated with its $100 taxable loss that reduced Exelon's consolidated tax liability. Due to that cash reimbursement, BGE's regulatory books and financial statements (prepared in accordance with Generally Accepted Accounting Principles, or "GAAP"), would no longer reflect a tax asset associated with the $100 loss.

**Q. What is the methodology prescribed in the June 28, 2024, PLRs?**

A. In the PLRs, the IRS concluded that an individual utility, for ratemaking purposes only, should reflect the utilization of its tax net operating losses, which are attributable to accelerated tax depreciation deductions, in future years (or within the carry-back period) when it has sufficient separate company taxable income, irrespective of the income of the consolidated group or other group members.

The "normalization" rules are intended to ensure that regulated utilities fully utilize tax law provisions that were designed to encourage capital expenditures. The IRS concluded that a regulated utility that reduces its tax net operating losses to the extent those tax losses can be utilized on consolidated return basis to offset the tax liability of another affiliated group member does not comply with the "normalization" rules.

The IRS mandates this ratemaking treatment even if a utility's tax net operating losses are used to reduce taxable income of other entities in a consolidated group, the utility is paid for the use of its losses through an intercompany tax sharing agreement, and the GAAP financial statements reflect that reimbursement.

**Q. What are the ratemaking implications associated with this corrected methodology?**

A. For ratemaking purposes, the corrected methodology prescribed in the PLRs requires BGE to make two ratemaking adjustments that reduce: (1) accumulated deferred income taxes ("ADIT"); and (2) the income tax regulatory liability included in rate base. Each of these adjustments result in an increase to rate base.

**Q. What is ADIT?**

A. ADIT reflects the temporary differences between when an expense (or revenue) is recognized in a company's financial and accounting records, commonly referred to as a company's "books," versus when the company recognizes that expense or (revenue) on its tax return. Deferred income taxes relate to future tax effects and can be classified as either deferred income tax liabilities or deferred income tax assets.

A deferred tax liability ("DTL") (i.e., a future tax liability) occurs when BGE realizes the tax benefit before it recognizes the item for financial accounting purposes. When this happens, BGE generally reduces rate base because the funds are not investor supplied. Conversely, a deferred tax asset ("DTA") (i.e., a future tax benefit such as an NOLC) occurs when BGE realizes the tax benefit after it recognizes the item for financial accounting purposes. When this happens, BGE generally increases rate base.

**Q. What adjustment is required to BGE's ADIT and rate base due to the NOLC methodology prescribed in the PLRs?**

A. For ratemaking purposes, the NOLC methodology prescribed in the PLRs requires BGE to reflect in ADIT incremental deferred tax assets associated with NOLCs, attributable to accelerated tax depreciation, that have not been used to offset its separate company taxable income (even if those tax net operating losses were utilized by the consolidated group and reimbursed through the tax sharing agreement). This incremental deferred tax asset related to using the corrected NOLC methodology prescribed by the IRS reduces ADIT and consequently increases average rate base by approximately $35 million as of March 31, 2026.

**Q. What adjustment is required to BGE's rate base regarding the income tax regulatory liability?**

A. The NOLC methodology prescribed in the PLRs also requires BGE to adjust its existing regulatory liability associated with excess deferred income taxes ("EDIT"). Specifically, BGE is required to reduce the portion of EDIT related to accelerated depreciation (i.e., protected) that was recorded in connection with the corporate rate change in the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act ("TCJA") that would have been reflected in a tax net operating loss at the time of the law change.

EDIT represents the difference between the ADIT at the prior federal corporate tax rate of 35% and the new tax rate of 21%. On the date of enactment of the TCJA, BGE established a regulatory liability for the excess deferred taxes that will be passed back to customers in future rates. The corrected NOLC methodology reduces ADIT and thus EDIT on the date of enactment of the TCJA. As a result, BGE's existing regulatory liability related to EDIT must be adjusted, resulting in an increase to average rate base of approximately $45.3 million as of March 31, 2026.

**Q. Does this corrected methodology impact EDIT amortization?**

A. Yes. The corrected methodology requires an adjustment to protected property-related EDIT, which is amortized using the Average Rate Assumption Method ("ARAM") as required by the "normalization" provisions under federal tax law.[^61] Under this corrected methodology, BGE has less ADIT at the date of the corporate rate change (i.e., December 31, 2017), thereby adjusting the starting point for calculating EDIT. As previously explained, BGE is required to reduce the portion of EDIT related to accelerated depreciation that was recorded in connection with the TCJA corporate rate change that would have been reflected in a NOLC at the time of the law change. As a result, the corrected methodology reduced the EDIT amortization by approximately $1.5 million for the twelve months ended March 31, 2026.

**Q. Do BGE's GAAP financial statements reflect ADIT associated with the federal NOLC?**

A. BGE's GAAP financial statements do not reflect a deferred tax asset associated with a federal NOLC. As previously noted, BGE calculates and pays tax based on its separate company books and records. BGE is paid for any excess tax losses or other tax attributes (e.g., tax credits) to the extent the excess tax losses or tax attributes can be utilized in the consolidated return to offset the tax liability of another subsidiary. For the applicable test period, BGE was paid cash for the use of its excess tax losses and thus did not report a deferred tax asset associated with federal NOLC on its GAAP financial statements.

**Q. Have you prepared a detailed analysis of the impact of the NOLCs on BGE's ADIT and EDIT balances which are reflected in Maryland electric distribution rate base?**

A. Yes. A detailed analysis of the impact of the NOLCs on BGE's ADIT and EDIT balances which are reflected in its electric distribution rate base was completed.

**Q. When did BGE first reflect the corrected methodology described in the PLRs?**

A. For ratemaking purposes, BGE first reflected the impacts of the corrected methodology described in the PLRs in its 2024 Annual Information Filing ("2024 AIF").[^62] BGE also reflected the impacts of the correct methodology described in the PLRs in BGE's 2025 Annual Information Filing ("2025 AIF").[^63]

**Q. Did the inclusion of the NOLC adjustment cure the normalization violation?**

A. Yes. The inclusion of the NOLC adjustment necessary to comply with the "normalization" rules in BGE's 2024 AIF remedied the "normalization" violation and avoided draconian penalties that would result in higher customer rates associated with the loss of accelerated depreciation for tax purposes. Since the normalization violation was cured, BGE can continue to claim accelerated depreciation for tax purposes.

**Q. What happens if the NOLC adjustment is disallowed in this proceeding?**

A. If the NOLC adjustment is disallowed in this proceeding, BGE will be in violation of the "normalization" rules and subject to penalties, including the loss of accelerated depreciation for tax purposes, a draconian impact for both BGE and its customers.

### B. Corporate Alternative Minimum Tax

**Q. How did the Inflation Reduction Act ("IRA")[^64] impact BGE?**

A. The IRA implemented a new corporate alternative minimum tax ("CAMT") beginning in 2023 that imposes a 15% tax on adjusted financial statement income. Corporations, including Exelon and its subsidiaries, now pay the greater of 15% of adjusted financial statement income or their regular federal tax liability. Beginning in 2023, based on available tax guidance, Exelon and its subsidiaries including BGE, were each subject to CAMT.

**Q. For CAMT purposes, how was the adjusted financial statement income determined?**

A. The starting point for calculating adjusted financial statement income is GAAP financial statement net income. Pursuant to the federal tax statute, GAAP financial statement net income is adjusted for federal income taxes, accelerated tax depreciation, pension and other postemployment benefits.[^65]

**Q. How does BGE calculate the CAMT?**

A. BGE calculates the CAMT based on its standalone separate company books and records.

**Q. Why did BGE's CAMT exceed its regular tax liability?**

A. Certain expenditures that qualify for accelerated tax deductions in the calculation of a corporation's regular tax liability, such as tax repairs and certain overhead costs, are required to be capitalized for financial statement purposes. Prior to the issuance of new guidance by the U.S. Department of Treasury and the IRS discussed later in my testimony, these expenditures are also required to be capitalized and depreciated for purposes of calculating CAMT rather than immediately deducted. As a result, BGE's regular tax liability has been lower than its CAMT liability.

**Q. How does BGE account for the CAMT in its financial statements?**

A. For income tax accounting purposes, a current income tax liability and current income tax expense will be recorded for the CAMT liability but will be equally offset by recording a deferred tax asset and a reduction to deferred income tax expense to reflect the CAMT credit carryforward. As a result, there is no net incremental income tax expense associated with the CAMT. Corporations are entitled to a CAMT tax credit equal to the amount by which the CAMT liability exceeds the regular tax liability. This amount may not be carried back to prior years but can be carried forward indefinitely and used in future years when the regular tax liability exceeds the CAMT liability. For income tax accounting purposes, a deferred tax asset is established for the CAMT credit carryforward.

**Q. What is the ratemaking impact associated with CAMT?**

A. BGE includes the deferred tax asset associated with the CAMT credit carryforward as an increase to rate base.[^66]

**Q. Why is it appropriate to include the deferred tax asset related to CAMT in rate base?**

A. Accelerated tax deductions related to utility property generate cash tax benefits that reduce the cost of capital. For rate making purposes, rate base is adjusted downward to reflect the lower cost of capital attributable to accelerated tax deductions. The CAMT has the opposite effect; it reduces the realization of the tax benefits associated with accelerated tax deductions, such as tax repairs and certain other overhead capitalization adjustments that are not deductible in the calculation of CAMT. The ADIT associated with tax repairs and other property-related tax adjustments are included in BGE's rate base as a downward adjustment. Therefore, to reflect the appropriate cost of capital, the deferred tax asset associated with the CAMT credit carryforward should likewise be included in rate base as an adjustment that offsets ADIT.

**Q. How does the corrected NOLC methodology impact the CAMT calculation?**

A. As discussed previously in my testimony, the IRS published a series of PLRs in 2024 that concluded that a utility, for ratemaking purposes only, should reflect the utilization of its tax net operating losses, which are attributable to accelerated tax depreciation deductions, in future years (or within the carry-back period) when it has sufficient separate company taxable income, irrespective of the income of the consolidated group.[^67]

The IRS mandates this ratemaking treatment even if a utility's tax net operating losses are used to reduce taxable income of other entities in a consolidated group and the utility is paid for the use of its losses through an intercompany tax sharing agreement and the GAAP financial statements reflect that reimbursement.

Prior to the issuance of new guidance by the U.S. Department of Treasury discussed later in my testimony, NOLCs arising in pre-CAMT years were deducted in the calculation of the regular tax liability but were not deductible for CAMT causing regular tax liability to be lower than CAMT. To the extent BGE had tax NOLCs available, they would be used to reduce regular tax liability but not CAMT. That difference between BGE's regular tax liability and CAMT increases the CAMT credit carryforward and the associated deferred tax asset.

**Q. Was the ratemaking treatment for CAMT approved in the prior Multi-Year Plan?**

A. Yes. The inclusion of BGE's CAMT credit carryforward as an increase to rate base was an uncontested issue in BGE's second Multi-year Rate Plan proceeding (Case No. 9692).

**Q. Has the U.S. Department of Treasury and the IRS issued any new CAMT guidance that impacts BGE?**

A. Yes. The U.S. Department of Treasury and the IRS issued CAMT guidance in the form of two separate notices: Notice 2025-49 issued on September 30, 2025, and Notice 2026-7 issued on February 18, 2026. The newly issued guidance provides for adjustments to the CAMT calculation that eliminates BGE's CAMT liability and the corresponding deferred tax asset associated with the CAMT credit carryforward.

Under Notice 2025-49, taxpayers are permitted to reduce their CAMT liability for pre-CAMT NOLCs (i.e., NOLCs arising in tax years prior to 2020) that are attributable to accelerated tax depreciation. As a result, under the corrected NOLC methodology, for ratemaking purposes, BGE has pre-CAMT NOLCs, attributable to accelerated tax depreciation, that can be utilized to reduce its CAMT liability.

Notice 2026-7 permits regulated utilities a repairs deduction equal to the one claimed for regular tax purposes in the calculation of the CAMT.

The combination of now being permitted to deduct pre-CAMT NOLCs related to accelerated tax depreciation and more importantly to claim a deduction for tax repair expenditures equal to the one claimed for regular tax purposes will mitigate BGE's CAMT liability and reduce the related CAMT tax credit carryforward. As a result, BGE's CAMT liability and the corresponding deferred tax asset related to CAMT credit carryforwards were eliminated.

The reduction in BGE's CAMT liability and the corresponding deferred tax asset related to the CAMT credit carryforwards reduces rate base resulting in a lower revenue requirement. The reduction in CAMT liability also improves BGE's financial credit metrics resulting in lower borrowing costs and increasing the availability of capital needed for investments in critical electric infrastructure.

**Q. How did BGE reflect the impact related to the new CAMT guidance?**

A. As previously noted, due to the issuance of Notice 2026-7, BGE's CAMT liability was eliminated, and BGE removed the corresponding deferred tax asset related to CAMT credit carryforwards in February 2026. Because Notice 2026-7 was not issued until February 18, 2026, BGE reported a deferred tax asset related to the CAMT credit carryforwards as an increase to rate base for the preceding ten months. To annualize and convert partial-period results into full-year equivalents, BGE is proposing a ratemaking adjustment (RMA) to remove the deferred tax asset related to the CAMT credit carryforward reported in the test period months March 31, 2025, through January 31, 2026, to fully eliminate the impacts of CAMT from rate base. The adjustment decreased BGE's average rate base by approximately $56.5 million as of March 31, 2026, and lowered the revenue requirement by approximately $6.2 million for the twelve months ended March 31, 2026.

## XIII. Summary of Revenue Requirement Exhibits

**Q. Have you prepared any revenue requirement exhibits and supporting schedules, Mr. Frain?**

A. Yes. As I mentioned earlier in my testimony, I have prepared Company Exhibits JCF-4 through JCF-6.[^68] The revenue requirement exhibits provided in this filing include:

Company Exhibit JCF-4 presents the calculations of the electric distribution base rate adjustments sought by the Company in this proceeding based on the historic test year for the twelve months ended March 31, 2026.

Company Exhibit JCF-5 shows the derivation of electric distribution rate base and operating income on an unadjusted and adjusted basis based on the twelve months ended March 31, 2026. This schedule also includes the capital structure and rate of return, the unadjusted and adjusted electric distribution returns based on the foregoing amounts, and a revenue requirement summary.

Company Exhibit JCF-6 includes a listing of the Company's adjustments to electric distribution operating income and rate base, as well as the revenue requirement effect of each. Pro forma adjustments are being presented to reflect Commission precedent as well as certain other adjustments applicable to the rate-effective period which are known and measurable. Separate schedules detailing the computation of pro forma adjustments are also provided. Company Witness McCart will be addressing Operating Income Adjustments 1 through 18 as well as Operating Income Adjustment 37 and Rate Base Adjustments 1 through 6 in her direct testimony. I will be addressing Operating Income Adjustments 19 through 36 and Rate Base Adjustments 7 through 18 in my direct testimony.[^69]

**Q. What test period is the Company's rate filing covering?**

A. As I stated earlier in my testimony, the Company's demonstrated need in this proceeding is based on actual results for adjusted electric distribution operating income and adjusted electric distribution rate base for the test period covering the twelve months ended March 31, 2026.

**Q. Can you please testify to the accuracy of the financial statements upon which your case is based?**

A. The actual financial statement results which serve as the basis for this rate adjustment request as presented on Company Exhibits JCF-4 through JCF-6 are complete and accurate. Under the Sarbanes-Oxley Act of 2002 (the "Act"), senior management has certified BGE's Securities and Exchange Commission ("SEC") quarterly filings on Form 10-Q and its 2025 annual filing on Form 10-K under Section 302 and 906 of the Act. In addition, PricewaterhouseCoopers ("PwC"), the Company's independent external auditor, issued a clean, unqualified opinion for the Company's 2025 financial statements for both SEC and FERC reporting purposes.

As a result of Section 404 of the Act, the Company identified, assessed, documented, and tested all key controls for every major process affecting BGE's financial statements. PwC also tested these controls in support of their audit of BGE and found no significant deficiencies or material weaknesses, and issued a clean, unqualified opinion to BGE with respect to internal controls over financial reporting. These attestations enhance the quality of this rate adjustment request as they support the Company's books and records that are the basis of this filing.

### A. Company Exhibit JCF-4

**Q. Please describe Company Exhibit JCF-4 – Electric Distribution Revenue Requirement Summary.**

A. Company Exhibit JCF-4, entitled "Electric Distribution Revenue Requirement Summary," shows the step-by-step determination of the base rate revenues the Company is requesting in this proceeding.

I will now take the opportunity to describe the step-by-step calculation. First, the Company's required income (Line 3) is calculated by multiplying the adjusted rate base (Line 1) by the rate of return (Line 2) supported in the Direct Testimonies of Company Witnesses Cloyd and McKenzie.[^70] Second, I compare the amount on Line 3 to the adjusted operating income (Line 4), which results in an operating income deficiency (Line 5).[^71] Third, this deficiency is "grossed up" for the additional taxes that will be paid as a result of the increased revenues and also the uncollectible factor, a practice consistent with Commission precedent (Line 6).[^72] Line 7 reflects the additional revenues needed to provide the Company an opportunity to earn the proposed rate of return on the Company's adjusted average distribution rate base after recovering all prudently incurred costs and all applicable taxes.

### B. Company Exhibit JCF-5

**Q. Please describe Company Exhibit JCF-5 - Rate Base and Operating Income Summary.**

A. Company Exhibit JCF-5, entitled "Electric Distribution Rate Base and Operating Income Summary" shows the Company's electric distribution average rate base, operating income, and return for the Company's historic test year, the twelve months ended March 31, 2026. These schedules also include the capital structure and a revenue requirement summary.

The first section, Rate Base (Average Basis), shows each major component of electric distribution rate base (Lines 2 through 11) consistent with the Commission's previous findings. Rate base represents the amount of investment financed by investors that is used and useful in providing utility service to customers. All of the components of unadjusted rate base shown are 13-month average balances, with the exception of cash working capital which is computed using the applicable years' operating expense levels. The unadjusted cash working capital calculation is presented in Company Exhibit NM-2. Column 1 presents rate base on an unadjusted basis. Column 2 reflects the impact of the various rate base ratemaking adjustments, and Column 3 presents rate base on an adjusted basis, which is computed by applying the ratemaking adjustments to the unadjusted balances.

In the operating income section, the Company's electric distribution operating expenses (Lines 17 through 25) are deducted from electric distribution operating revenue (Line 16). The allowance for funds used during construction ("AFUDC", line 26) is then added to this amount, and interest on customer deposits (line 27) is deducted. These two items are included because their corresponding balance sheet accounts (i.e., Construction Work In Progress ("CWIP") and the customer deposits liability) are included in the calculation of rate base. Line 28 provides the resulting operating income. Consistent with the rate base section above, columns are shown for the unadjusted amounts, ratemaking adjustments, and adjusted amounts.

Lines 29 through 32 present the capital structure, rate of return (including cost of debt and proposed return on equity), and related weighted cost of capital being requested in this proceeding, which are supported by Company Witnesses Cloyd and McKenzie in their respective Direct Testimonies.[^73]

Lines 33 through 38 provide the unadjusted and adjusted rate of return and return on equity.[^74]

The final section of Company Exhibit JCF-5 summarizes the revenue requirement based on the test year. The Company's required operating income is calculated by multiplying the adjusted rate base by the rate of return supported by the capital structure section (Lines 29-32) discussed above. The required operating income amount is then compared to the adjusted operating income (labelled "Pro forma Operating Income" on line 43 of the exhibit), which results in an operating income deficiency (Line 44). This deficiency is "grossed up" for both the additional taxes that will be paid as a result of the increased revenues as well as an uncollectible factor (Line 45 – the "Revenue Conversion Factor"), a practice consistent with Commission precedent, to arrive at a Revenue Requirement Deficiency amount (Line 46).[^75]

**Q. What is the basis of the amounts comprising the rate base and operating income included on Company Exhibit JCF-5?**

A. The amounts comprising the operating income and rate base for the test year reflect actual electric distribution revenues, expenses, and assets per the general ledger of BGE.

**Q. Does this filing reflect any changes to rate base and operating income since the Company's last rate case filing?**

A. Yes. The Electric Plant (Line 2) of rate base now reflects the balance of CWIP, and likewise, operating income was revised to reflect the AFUDC related to CWIP as noted on Line 26 of Company Exhibit JCF-5. These changes were made given that the instant base rate filing is based on a historic test year rather than a forecasted test year. In Order No. 89678, Case No. 9645, the Commission acknowledged that utilities were previously allowed to include CWIP in rate base with AFUDC in operating income to mitigate regulatory lag. The Commission justified the inclusion of CWIP in rate base in a historic test year rate filing because in traditional historic rate cases, projects started in the test year would typically be completed by the rate effective year, such that a majority of the test year CWIP would be in service and used and useful by that time.[^76] Given that the current filing is a traditional historic test year and not a forecasted test year, the Company is including CWIP in rate base and AFUDC in operating income, consistent with the clear Commission guidance in Order No. 89678.

**Q. Are there any other electric-related amounts that are not reflected in adjusted rate base and operating income as presented in this filing?**

A. Yes. Actual electric transmission revenues, expenses, and rate base are appropriately excluded from this filing as they are subject to the jurisdiction of the FERC. Also, actual revenues and expenses associated with electric commodity and recovered through the SOS charges, including SOS bad debt costs, are excluded from this filing. Lastly, the portion of actual electric distribution bad debt expense associated with changes in the Accounts Receivable reserve is excluded from this filing, consistent with long-standing Commission precedent.[^77]

**Q. Have you included any additional exhibits that further support the unadjusted rate base reflected in Company Exhibit JCF-5?**

A. Yes. I have prepared Company Exhibit JCF-7 which provides an in-service view of new additions to rate base by category and project which serves to support the unadjusted Plant in Service amounts reflected on Company Exhibit JCF-5 (Line 2). The Electric Plant line reflects both plant in service and CWIP amounts. I will discuss Company Exhibit JCF-7 in Section XIV of my testimony.

### C. Company Exhibit JCF-6

**Q. Please describe Company Exhibit JCF-6 – Electric Distribution Revenue Requirement (Unadjusted and Adjusted).**

A. Company Exhibit JCF-6, entitled "Electric Distribution Revenue Requirement (Unadjusted and Adjusted)," begins with the unadjusted rate base, operating income and revenue requirement amounts carried forward from Company Exhibit JCF-5. This exhibit then lists the various operating income and rate base adjustments applied to unadjusted operating income and rate base to arrive at the respective adjusted amounts and revenue requirement amount. Each of the adjustments is shown net of income tax effects, where appropriate, at the prevailing 27.5175% statutory tax rate, and a revenue requirement impact for each adjustment is also shown. I have also included supporting schedules for those ratemaking adjustments I am sponsoring (numbered to correspond to the schedule reference noted on this exhibit) showing the computation for each adjustment listed on Company Exhibit JCF-6.

**Q. Which ratemaking adjustments reflected in Company Exhibit JCF-6 will you be sponsoring?**

A. As I mentioned earlier in my testimony, Company Witness McCart will be sponsoring Operating Income Adjustments 1 through 18 as well as Operating Income Adjustment 37 which is the interest synchronization ratemaking adjustment. In addition, she will also be sponsoring the related Rate Base Adjustments 1 through 6.[^78] I will be sponsoring the remainder of the adjustments consisting of Operating Income Adjustment 19 through 36 and Rate Base Adjustments 7 through 18. For ease of reference, I have included an additional column to the right of the Schedule Reference column entitled "Witness" which notes who is responsible for each specific ratemaking adjustment.

**Q. Please walk through the ratemaking adjustments included on Company Exhibit JCF-6 you will be sponsoring beginning with Operating Income Adjustment 19.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 19 removes compensation for supervisors that exceeds 110% of the maximum annual salary payable to the chairman of the Commission for the same calendar year, in accordance with PUA §4-504, which was enacted through the 2026 Utility RELIEF Act.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 20 and Rate Base Adjustment 7 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 20 reflects the amortization associated with the two major outage events that occurred in August 2023 and February 2025. The incremental expenses associated with each event were deferred to a regulatory asset and are currently included in unadjusted rate base. In Case No. 9645, Order No. 89678, the Commission granted BGE's request to create a regulatory asset for major outage event expenses as it aligns recovery of major outage events with the actual incremental costs to restore service.[^79] Therefore, the Company is requesting recovery of these costs over a five-year period consistent with Commission Order No. 89678. Rate Base Adjustment 7 is the companion adjustment and reflects the rate base impact of the amortization of the deferred major outage event regulatory asset provided in Operating Income Adjustment 20.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 21 and Rate Base Adjustment 8 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 21 reflects the amortization associated with the 2023 minor outage event regulatory asset established in accordance with Order No. 92106 in Case No. 9645. In this order, the Commission directed that the incremental O&M expenses for minor storms (or outage events) be removed from the 2023 Final Reconciliation and be placed into a regulatory asset with a five-year recovery period, similar to major storm O&M.[^80] Rate Base Adjustment 8 is the companion adjustment and reflects the inclusion of this newly authorized regulatory asset in rate base as well as the rate base impact of the amortization of the deferred minor outage event regulatory asset provided in Operating Income Adjustment 21.

**Q. Why is it appropriate for the minor storm regulatory asset to be included in rate base?**

A. These minor storm costs incurred in 2023 were financed by investor-supplied capital at the time and therefore should be included in rate base consistent with the major outage event regulatory asset. In other words, cash has been advanced by investors to fund the restoration costs for these minor storms, but recovery from customers is still pending. As noted in the Commission's Order No. 92106, the Company's storm spending, including both major storms and minor storms for both capital and O&M, was prudent and the recovery of these costs is just and reasonable.[^81] Given that these are prudently incurred and necessary for the Company to provide safe and reliable service, the Company is requesting in this proceeding the inclusion of the minor storm regulatory asset in rate base until such time as the deferred amounts are fully recovered. These costs should not be treated any differently than the major outage event regulatory asset or any other cost of service amount. Furthermore, there is no basis for unrecovered, prudently incurred minor outage event restoration costs to be excluded from rate base.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 22 and Rate Base Adjustment 9 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 22 reflects the amortization associated with the 2023 conduit regulatory asset established in accordance with Commission Order No. 92106 in Case No. 9645. In this order, the Commission directed that certain 2023 conduit costs (O&M expense and depreciation expense) be placed into a regulatory asset until a BCA is filed and reviewed in BGE's next rate case.[^82] Given that the Company has demonstrated in this filing that the 2023 Conduit Agreement is cost-effective as supported by the BCA discussed in Section X above, the Company is requesting recovery of these costs over a five-year period consistent with other regulatory assets. Rate Base Adjustment 9 is the companion adjustment and reflects the inclusion of this newly authorized regulatory asset in rate base as well as the rate base impact of the amortization of the conduit regulatory asset provided in Operating Income Adjustment 22.

**Q. Can you please discuss Rate Base Adjustment 10 and Operating Income Adjustment 23 in Company Exhibit JCF-6?**

A. Rate Base Adjustment 10 provides for the inclusion of NOLC in rate base as discussed in Section XII above. Operating Income Adjustment 23 is the companion adjustment.

**Q. Can you please discuss Rate Base Adjustment 11 in Company Exhibit JCF-6?**

A. Rate Base Adjustment 11 eliminates the Company's CAMT liability, and the corresponding deferred tax asset related to CAMT credit carryforwards as discussed in Section XII above.

**Q. Can you please discuss Operating Income Adjustment 24 in Company Exhibit JCF-6?**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 24 reduces amortization expense as a result of House Bill 864 from the 2024 General Assembly session[^83] and Commission Order No. 91175 in Case No. 9075, which limited the return on the remaining regulatory asset balance for EmPOWER Maryland programs to the utility weighted average cost of debt, effective July 1, 2024. This adjustment increases operating income so that the requested revenue requirement in this case does not include an equity return on the remaining regulatory asset balance, contrary to the intent of the above noted legislation and Commission order.

**Q. Can you please discuss Operating Income Adjustment 25 in Company Exhibit JCF-6?**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 25 adjusts the electric distribution capital structure for HB 864 and Commission Order No. 91175. As a result of HB 864 and Commission Order No. 91175, the return on the remaining EmPOWER Maryland regulatory asset is limited to BGE's average cost of debt. As such, this operating income adjustment serves to adjust the rate of return for the amount of debt available to finance electric distribution rate base exclusive of EmPOWER Maryland, which is reduced due to Commission Order No. 91175. In other words, if the EmPOWER Maryland regulatory asset is assumed to be financed with only debt, that debt is no longer available to finance the rest of distribution rate base, and this adjustment applies the resultant higher rate of return to rate base excluding the EmPOWER Maryland regulatory asset.

**Q. Please summarize the difference between the two EmPOWER Maryland adjustments Operating Income 24 and Operating Income 25 described above.**

A. In summary, Operating Income Adjustment 24 ensures that the revenue request in this proceeding does not recover the equity portion of the return for EmPOWER Maryland contrary to HB 864 and Commission Order No. 91175, whereas Operating Income Adjustment 25 recognizes that if EmPOWER Maryland is 100 percent debt financed, the remaining rate base is financed by a higher percent of equity and, as a result, uses different capitalization ratios more weighted to equity.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 26 and Rate Base Adjustment 12 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 26 reflects amortization associated with BGE's Supplier Consolidated Billing ("SCB") regulatory asset. In Case No. 9461, Order No. 90046 (addressing SCB), the Commission authorized utilities to "seek recovery of consultant costs as part of a review of all other costs associated with implementing SCB. The utilities may establish a regulatory asset for any costs associated with Electric Data Interchange development as needed to recover consultant costs." Rate Base Adjustment 12 is the companion adjustment.

**Q. Please describe Rate Base Adjustment 13 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Rate Base Adjustment 13 establishes a second new SCB system costs regulatory asset for costs associated with the development of system capabilities to accommodate supplier consolidated billing. COMAR 20.53.05.07, COMAR 20.59.05.09, and Case No. 9461, Order No. 89116 require Maryland utilities to offer SCB to third-party energy providers. After incurring significant development costs, Senate Bill 1 ("SB 1") was enacted during the 2024 General Assembly session which significantly changed the residential competitive energy markets in Maryland.[^84] On July 12, 2024, the Commission issued Order No. 91220 which denied the request of the Exelon Utilities to pause continued work on SCB implementation in light of the SB 1 legislation. Subsequently, on December 11, 2024, in Case No. 9461, the Commission issued Order No. 91448 which directed utilities to "pause any new SCB implementation work until such time as retail suppliers notify the Commission of their intent to participate in the testing of SCB and their ongoing commitment to actively participate in SCB." Costs associated with these regulatory-mandated development activities for the purpose of SCB implementation are currently included in CWIP and are reflected in rate base. Given that these investments will never be placed in service and as a result, depreciation and related cost recovery as capital will never commence, the Company proposes to move these costs out of CWIP and into a regulatory asset account which will facilitate amortization and recovery in rates as the Company is seeking to recover these costs over a five-year period.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 27 and Rate Base Adjustment 14 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 27 reflects the amortization associated with the newly established SCB system costs regulatory asset established in Rate Base Adjustment 13. This adjustment reduces operating income by providing for amortization of the proposed SCB system costs regulatory asset as established in Rate Base Adjustment 13 over five years. Additionally, Rate Base Adjustment 14 is the companion adjustment reflecting the rate base impact of the amortization.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 28 and Rate Base Adjustment 15 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 28 reduces operating income to reflect the known level of depreciation expense, net of depreciation savings, for non-revenue producing safety and reliability plant placed in service through the end of the test year as of March 31, 2026, as provided in Rate Base Adjustment 15. This operating income adjustment, which was authorized by the Commission in prior traditional historic test year cases, provides for the known and measurable appropriate annual level of depreciation expense, net of depreciation savings, to be reflected in the rate-effective period.[^85] In addition to reflecting gross plant at the appropriate level, Rate Base Adjustment 15 also adjusts accumulated depreciation and related accumulated deferred income taxes to reflect rate base for these investments most accurately.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 29 and Rate Base Adjustment 16 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 29 reduces operating income to reflect the known level of depreciation expense, net of depreciation savings, for non-revenue producing safety and reliability plant placed in service from the end of the test year (March 31, 2026) through September 30, 2026, the most recently available month at the expected time of the evidentiary hearings in this proceeding, as provided in Rate Base Adjustment 16. This adjustment, which was authorized by the Commission in prior cases, provides for the appropriate annual level of depreciation expense, net of depreciation savings, to be reflected in the rate-effective period.[^86] Rate Base Adjustment 16 also adjusts accumulated depreciation and related accumulated deferred income taxes to reflect rate base for these investments most accurately. Both Operating Income Adjustment 29 and Rate Base Adjustment 16 will be updated for actual results through September 30, 2026, at the time of the hearings.

**Q. Why is it appropriate for the safety and reliability investments described above to be recovered on a terminal basis?**

A. The safety and reliability investments included above are essential to maintain the safety and reliability of the BGE electric distribution system and are non-revenue producing in nature. The terminalization of these necessary known and measurable investments as provided in Rate Base Adjustments 15 and 16 and Operating Income Adjustments 28 and 29 reflects a better matching of costs and rates and are needed to provide BGE with a reasonable opportunity to earn its authorized return. The combination of using a historic test period to set rates along with the reality of these necessary, known and certain safety and reliability investments will prevent the Company from earning its Commission-authorized return during the rate effective period absent these ratemaking adjustments. Since one of the foundations of the rate setting process is the matching principle, achieving just and reasonable rates necessitates a matching of customers' cost of service with distribution rates in the rate effective period. Recovery of these investments on a thirteen-month average basis subjects the Company to regulatory lag and will inevitably result in the under-recovery of authorized revenue levels. As such, the Company is proposing the above-noted terminalization adjustments to allow the Company an opportunity to earn its authorized rate of return on these essential investments.

**Q. Can you please discuss Operating Income Adjustment 30 in Company Exhibit JCF-6?**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 30 reduces operating income by providing for the effect on labor expense of the March 2026 general wage increase for BGE employees, which is not fully reflected in test year results. This adjustment is consistent with adjustments submitted by the Company and authorized by the Commission in numerous prior historic test year cases.[^87]

**Q. Can you please discuss Operating Income Adjustment 31 in Company Exhibit JCF-6?**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 31 reduces operating income to provide for the effect of general inflation on non-labor O&M expenses during the rate-effective period. This adjustment was authorized by the Commission in Case No. 9484 to help address regulatory lag given that historic test years do not reflect systemic inflation.[^88] This adjustment reflects a 4.64% inflation factor based on a five-year average of the CPI per the U.S. Department of Labor, Bureau of Labor Statistics, as authorized by the Commission in Case No. 9484, Order No. 88975.[^89]

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 32 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 32 provides for recovery of O&M expense related to the prepaid service program (BGE FlexPay) discussed in Section VIII of my testimony. Under this program which allows customers greater control over the affordability of their energy bills, customers can choose to prepay for their anticipated energy consumption, and through regular communication from BGE and self-service digital tools, can track how their usage is trending in relation to their pre-payment and add to their prepaid account as needed. This adjustment provides for the O&M expense to be incurred in the rate-effective period to implement this program.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 33 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. As discussed above in Section IX of my testimony as well as the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Wright, Operating Income Adjustment 33 reflects a level of incremental major outage event restoration O&M expense based on a five-year weighted average of these expenses.[^90] This adjustment provides for a level of incremental O&M major outage event restoration expense which serves as a base level proxy for major storm expenses in operating income for the rate-effective period. As discussed in Section IX of my testimony and the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Karas, to the extent that the Company incurs more or less storm expense than the level provided in Operating Income Adjustment 33, any difference will be recovered – or passed back to customers through the Storm Rider proposed in the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Karas.[^91]

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 34 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Similarly, Operating Income Adjustment 34 reflects a level of incremental non-major outage event restoration O&M expense based on a five-year weighted average of these expenses. Similar to major storms, this adjustment provides for a level of incremental O&M non-major outage event restoration expense which serves as a base level proxy for non-major storm expense in operating income for the rate-effective period. As discussed in Section IX of my testimony and the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Karas, to the extent that the Company incurs more or less incremental non-major storm expense than the level provided in Operating Income Adjustment 34, any difference will be recovered – or passed back to customers through the Storm Rider supported in the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Karas.[^92]

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 35 and Rate Base Adjustment 17 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 35 and Rate Base Adjustment 17 are placeholders in the event the conduit agreement renewal negotiations between Baltimore City and BGE result in additional conduit costs. As I note in Section X above, BGE is currently negotiating revised terms to the conduit agreement with Baltimore City. The periodic renewal of the conduit agreement with Baltimore City includes negotiation of the fees BGE is to pay for maintenance of the conduit system, and the City has historically sought to increase these fees upon each such renewal. To the extent that the negotiated contract results in incremental capital investment or O&M expenses through increased fees payable to the City, I am requesting regulatory asset treatment for the depreciation of the incremental capital and for any additional O&M expense. In the event that any costs are incurred during the course of this proceeding and before the hearings, the Company will update these adjustments as applicable. In the event incremental expenses are incurred and deferred into the conduit fee regulatory asset being proposed subsequent to the hearings in this proceeding, the Company will seek recovery in a future base rate proceeding.

**Q. Please describe Operating Income Adjustment 36 and Rate Base Adjustment 18 in Company Exhibit JCF-6.**

A. Operating Income Adjustment 36 and Rate Base Adjustment 18 are placeholders in the event the Company receives any Commission orders related to pending proceedings (such as Distribution battery program development costs) prior to the hearings.[^93] Since the above-referenced proceedings have not concluded as of the date of this filing, the Company is not requesting recovery of these specific programs at this time. However, to the extent that any Commission orders are issued during the course of this proceeding and before the hearings, the Company will update this adjustment as applicable.

## XIV. Review of Historic Capital Additions and Test Year O&M Expenses [source says "IV."]

**Q. What is the purpose of this section of your testimony?**

A. This section of my Direct Testimony, along with the Direct Testimonies of Company Witnesses Cloyd and Wright, demonstrates the prudency and necessity of the electric distribution costs underlying the requested revenue increase presented on Company Exhibit JCF-4, including certain capital expenditures that were excluded from the 2023 Final Reconciliation.[^94] In lieu of testimony, I have prepared two exhibits: Company Exhibit JCF-7, which provides additional detail on the historic capital ("plant") additions since the Company's last prudency review in the 2023 Final Reconciliation in Case No. 9645; and Company Exhibit JCF-8, which summarizes the historic O&M expenses from the test year by FERC Account. I will discuss these exhibits in more detail in the following questions. These exhibits, together with the category descriptions presented in the Direct Testimonies of Company Witnesses Cloyd and Wright and the applicable Project Authorization Presentations, demonstrate that these costs were both prudent and necessary to provide safe and reliable service to our customers.[^95]

The National Regulatory Research Institute advises that the prudence standard revolves around the question of whether an action is reasonable given the facts that are known and knowable at the time that the decision is made. If a utility is able to demonstrate that a decision to pursue a particular investment was reasonable at the time it was made, even though the eventual outcome was not as initially desired, the utility is still allowed to recover its costs.[^96] Under this standard, the Company believes that all costs underlying this rate adjustment were prudently incurred and beneficial to customers. It also believes it has demonstrated its burden of proof through the testimonies and accompanying exhibits discussed above.

**Q. Please describe Company Exhibit JCF-7.**

A. Company Exhibit JCF-7, entitled "Electric Distribution Plant Additions - Summary by Category and Project," shows additions to electric distribution rate base since the Company's last prudency review in the 2023 Final Reconciliation in Case No. 9645. Therefore, the exhibit supports electric distribution in-service additions during the period January 2024 through March 2026. The Company has provided details including problem, solution, and benefit statements at the project level supporting at least 80% of electric distribution additions and 25% of projects.[^97] To provide additional detail and transparency, the applicable CONFIDENTIAL and CONFIDENTIAL CEII Project Authorization Presentations[^98] for the projects shown in Company Exhibit JCF-7, that were subject to the Project Authorization Process explained by Company Witness Wright, will be uploaded and accessible to the parties on a SharePoint site that BGE will set up for this matter.[^99] Company Witnesses Cloyd and Wright, who are co-sponsoring Company Exhibit JCF-7, discuss each applicable non-operational and operational category, respectively, in their Direct Testimonies.[^100]

**Q. Please describe Company Exhibit JCF-8.**

A. Company Exhibit JCF-8, entitled "Electric Distribution Operations and Maintenance Expenses - Summary by FERC Account," displays the actual O&M expenses embedded in the Company's unadjusted O&M expenses for the historic test year by the FERC Uniform System of Accounts.

**Q. Mr. Frain, is there anything you would like to say about the capital projects where costs were excluded from the 2023 Final Reconciliation in Case No. 9645?**

A. Yes. In Order No. 92106, the Commission did not allow BGE to recover certain costs through the 2023 Final Reconciliation. For many of the projects excluded from the 2023 Final Reconciliation, the Commission noted that BGE did not demonstrate the standard of prudency for certain cost variances, particularly given the overspending that occurred across BGE's distribution system in 2023. While the Commission ultimately excluded these cost variances from the 2023 Final Reconciliation, it is important to note that this does not mean that they deemed these projects imprudent or unnecessary. The Commission made clear that, "BGE is not precluded from requesting certain disallowed adjustments if prudency is demonstrated in future rate cases."[^101]

The Company understands it has the burden of demonstrating the prudency and necessity of all costs, and will demonstrate this, as well as the value that these projects offer our customers, as part of this proceeding. The Company believes these costs were prudently incurred, despite the associated cost variances in 2023. The amounts currently in-service for these capital projects are inherently included in the requested rate base and revenue requirement as the costs were prudently incurred, and the infrastructure continues to be used, useful, and beneficial to provide safe and reliable service for customers. Further details supporting the prudency of certain electric distribution capital projects that were excluded from the 2023 Final Reconciliation in Case No. 9645 are included in the Direct Testimonies of Company Witnesses Wright and Cloyd, separated by operational and non-operational categories, respectively.[^102]

## XV. Conclusion

**Q. Do you have any final remarks that you would like to make?**

A. Yes. As supported in my testimony above, the Company is asking that the Commission grant BGE's requested electric distribution revenue requirement increase of $156.1 million based on a fair return of 7.68%.

**Q. Are there any other explicit approvals with respect to the revenue requirement that you are requesting the Commission provide to the Company?**

A. The Company respectfully requests the Commission's approval for the following:[^103]

1. A rate of return for its electric operations of 7.68% as proposed by Company Witness Cloyd, based on the Company's 4.72% embedded cost of debt, as well as the 10.4% ROE for electric, as recommended by Company Witness Adrien M. McKenzie;
2. Recovery of incremental major outage event expenses which were incurred in August 2023 and February 2025, with recovery over a five-year period consistent with Commission Order No. 89678 as provided in Operating Income Adjustment 20 and Rate Base Adjustment 7. The incremental cost of each event has been deferred in a regulatory asset pursuant to Case No. 9645, Order No. 89678;
3. Recovery of incremental 2023 minor outage event expenses over a five-year period consistent with Commission Order No. 92106 and inclusion of the regulatory asset in rate base (Operating Income Adjustment 21 and Rate Base Adjustment 8). The 2023 incremental minor outage event expenses have been deferred in a regulatory asset pursuant to Order No. 92106 in Case No. 9645;
4. Recovery of the 2023 conduit costs over a five-year period (Operating Income Adjustment 22 and Rate Base Adjustment 9) consistent with the existing conduit contract term and inclusion of this regulatory asset in rate base given that the 2023 Conduit Agreement has been proven cost-effective. The 2023 conduit costs have been deferred in a regulatory asset pursuant to Order No. 92106 in Case No. 9645;
5. Recovery of the supplier-consolidated billing ("SCB") consultant regulatory asset authorized in Case No. 9461, Order No. 90046 over a five-year period as provided in Operating Income Adjustment 26 and Rate Base Adjustment 12;
6. Establishment of the SCB system costs regulatory asset (Rate Base Adjustment 13) and the associated recovery over five years (Operating Income Adjustment 27 and Rate Base Adjustment 14) for which the SCB implementation work was paused by the Commission in Order No. 91448;
7. Approval of the BGE FlexPay program, the proposed prepaid service program, and waiver of the regulations, orders and Electric Tariff provisions set forth in Exhibit JCF-2A as well as related recovery of O&M expense to be incurred in the rate-effective period related to BGE FlexPay (Operating Income Adjustment 32);
8. Current recovery of incremental major and non-major outage event restoration expenses based on a five-year weighted average of such expenses (Operating Income Adjustments 33 and 34), with the ability to capture any difference between the level included in rates and the level actually incurred in the proposed Storm Rider detailed in the Direct Testimony of Company Witness Karas, which would then be recovered – or refunded – to customers in a separate rate filing for the proposed Storm Rider; and
9. Recovery of certain capital costs that were excluded as part of the 2023 Final Reconciliation but were not precluded from cost recovery in the future, as supported by Company Witnesses Cloyd and Wright.

**Q. Does this conclude your Direct Testimony?**

A. Yes.

The Court specifically rejected OPC's theories that the improvements should be treated as leasehold expenses rather than long-lived utility assets, finding substantial evidence supporting the Commission's determination that BGE's capital investments are depreciable over the life of the assets given BGE's perpetual access rights to the conduit system.
