# Company Exhibit JCF-2A: Waiver of Billing-related and Termination-related COMAR, Tariff, and Commission Orders

**Source pages**: PDF pages 104-106 (Exhibit pages 1-3 of 3)

### Waiver of Billing-related COMAR, Tariff, and Commission Orders

Given the differences in how prepaid operates compared with standard pay, BGE would need waivers of the following billing-related tariff and Commission requirements:

| Topic                 | Description                                                                                                                                              | Reason for Waiver                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Equivalent Customer Protection in Prepaid                                                                        |
| --------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Billing Period        | Section 7.2 (sending of a monthly bill), 7.3 (setting forth a net payment period of 20 days), 7.7 (that a customer may receive a consolidated bill).     | BGE will not render monthly bills under the program.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | The monthly statement will provide the customer with his or her billing information.                             |
| Budget Billing – EUSP | In Order No. 79535 in Case No. 8738, the Commission directed utilities to apply budget billing for customers receiving EUSP.                             | Budget billing will not be offered under the prepaid pilot. Budget billing is a program in which a customer pays a set amount each month, subject to periodic adjustments. Budget billing is not compatible with prepaid because each customer will determine the budgeted amount and frequency of the payments they prefer. | Prepaid will allow the customer to align their usage with their personal lifestyle and preferences.              |
| Budget Billing – USPP | COMAR 20.31.05.06 requires that customers on USPP be on an equal monthly payment plan.                                                                   | Budget billing is a program in which a customer pays a set amount each month, subject to periodic adjustments. Budget billing is not compatible with prepaid because each customer will determine the budgeted amount and frequency of the payments they prefer.                                                             | Prepaid will allow the customer to align their usage and payments with their personal lifestyle and preferences. |
| Reconnection Fee      | BGE Electric Retail Service Tariff Section 2.41 states that BGE will assess a reconnection fee of $20-$70, where service is discontinued for non-payment | BGE will waive reconnection fees under prepaid to acknowledge the lack of many collection-related expenses that prepaid customers cause prior to a disconnection, compared with standard pay customers.                                                                                                                      | Customers on prepaid are exempted.                                                                               |

### Waiver of Termination-related COMAR Provisions and Commission Orders

Prepaid disconnections should be treated like other customer-initiated, voluntary disconnections such as when customers move premises. Customers voluntarily choosing to enroll in prepaid understand that termination can occur when they permit their prepaid account balance to fall to zero. Furthermore, prepaid customers will receive frequent communications about their account balance via their chosen communication methods. BGE nonetheless requests a waiver of the termination-related Commission orders and requirements under COMAR 20.31.01.01C as follows:

| Topic                                                    | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Reason for Waiver                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Equivalent Customer Protection under Prepaid                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Termination – Third Party Notification                   | COMAR 20.31.01.07 requires a utility to inform its customers of the availability of third-party notification, by which the customer can designate a third person to receive termination notices.                                                                                                                                                               | Because customers manage their own account balance and days remaining preferences, prepaid does not contemplate the sending of a standard termination notice.                                                                                                                                                                          | Upon request, BGE will send via phone call, email and/or text messages notices regarding the account balance, including when the balance is nearing $0, to any party the customer chooses.                                                                                                             |
| Terminations – Insufficient Reasons for Termination      | COMAR 20.31.02.01B prohibits a utility from terminating a customer for a bill that is less than $100 and delinquent for less than 3 months.                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Under prepaid, customers voluntarily choose to participate in a service that prevents arrearages by allowing disconnections to occur at a zero balance.                                                                                                                                                                                | Customers who prefer the requirements of this section may transfer back to standard pay at any time with no penalty.                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Terminations - Notice                                    | COMAR 20.31.02.05-.06 requires a utility to send a notice of termination in-person or via mail at least 14 days before the date on which termination will occur.                                                                                                                                                                                               | Because customers manage their own account balance and days remaining preferences, prepaid does not contemplate the sending of a standard termination notice.                                                                                                                                                                          | Under prepaid, BGE will send communications, including account balance and estimated days remaining, more frequently compared with standard pay, and by the methods preferred by the customer.                                                                                                         |
| Terminations – Elderly and Handicapped Restrictions      | COMAR 20.31.03.02 requires that a utility make two personal contacts prior to termination for these customers.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | By enrolling, the customer is agreeing that notifications about impending disconnections will occur by phone call, email and/or text message, as the customer prefers. Thus, these notifications substitute for the disconnection notices as defined by COMAR.                                                                         | BGE will send notices via phone calls, emails and/or text messages advising the customer of the balance and when the balance is nearing zero. Thus, there will be BGE-initiated contacts prior to termination, via the methods customers prefer.                                                       |
| Terminations – Winter Restrictions                       | COMAR 20.31.03.03 prohibits a utility from terminating service to occupants of residential buildings for nonpayment of bills unless, inter alia, an affidavit is filed with the PSC that the termination does not constitute a threat to the life or health of the occupants, that two personal contacts have been made, and the arrearage must be above $300. | By enrolling, the customer is agreeing that service will be terminated remotely when the balance falls to zero. Thus, the filing of an affidavit on the health and safety of the occupants should not be required.                                                                                                                     | BGE will send notices via phone calls, emails and/or text messages advising the customer of the balance and when the balance is nearing zero. Thus, there will be BGE-initiated contacts prior to termination. BGE will also observe the weather restrictions under Public Utilities Article §7-301.1. |
| Terminations – Utility Service Protection Program (USPP) | COMAR 20.31.05 requires that USPP customers have an equal monthly payment plan, that a customer can only be terminated for nonpayment of two consecutive bills, and a utility must notify the customer if a payment is past due.                                                                                                                               | Under a prepaid program, customers would not receive bills, nor find themselves in a situation where two bills are past-due. Due to the nature of prepaid, budget billing is not compatible with the program.                                                                                                                          | BGE will send notices via phone calls, emails and/or text messages advising the customer of the balance and when the balance is nearing zero. Thus, there will be BGE-initiated contacts prior to termination.                                                                                         |
| Terminations – 55 day rule                               | In Order No. 80307 in Case No. 8919, the Commission adopted a settlement that requires utilities to withhold termination for a 55-day period for customers who apply for energy assistance within 14 days of the issuance of a termination notice.                                                                                                             | A 55-day hold will cause the customer to risk incurring an arrearage, which is inconsistent with one of the primary benefits many customers cite when choosing prepaid service.                                                                                                                                                        | Customers who prefer the requirements of this section may transfer back to standard pay at any time with no penalty.                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Terminations – Field Payments                            | In a March 12, 2009 Letter Order, the Commission directed BGE to continue the practice of accepting payment at the door.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | This practice is unworkable because some prepaid customers may choose to keep only a few days' worth of energy in their account, potentially requiring home visits every day. Prepaid customers choose to receive their account balance notifications via the methods they prefer, including phone calls, emails and/or text messages. | Instead of home visits prior to disconnection, BGE will send more frequent and timely notifications advising the customer of the balance and when the balance is nearing zero.                                                                                                                         |

# Company Exhibit JCF-2B: BGE Prepaid Power Terms & Conditions

**Source pages**: PDF pages 107-109 (Exhibit pages 1-3 of 3)

> **PILOT PROGRAM**
>
> ### BGE Prepaid Power Terms & Conditions

#### Overview

The Baltimore Gas and Electric Prepaid Power Program is being offered as a 12-month pilot program. Under the program:

- Advance payment is required to maintain gas and electric service.
- Funds that you prepay into your account are reduced based on an estimate of the cost of energy used each day.
- You will not receive a bill with an amount due or a due date; instead you will receive a monthly statement with a summary of your activities.
- You will also receive regular notifications with your prepaid account balance and an estimate of the days remaining until your funds run out.
- If you do not make the necessary payment after your account balance has reached zero, your power will stop.

> **EXTREMELY IMPORTANT:**
>
> Your service may be disconnected as soon as one business day after your account balance reaches zero (though not outside weekday business hours). Your service will be restored the same day if BGE receives your payment between Monday and Saturday by 6:00 p.m. that is enough to bring your balance to at least $15, including paying any debt that occurred if you used service after reaching a zero balance. Or, you can call BGE during business hours and ask to be restored and given a five-day extension of service. You can also request an extension before you are disconnected. Either way, once this extension expires you can avoid disconnection, or get reconnected, only by paying what is owed as described below, or returning to traditional postpay service.

- You must register your account online at BGE.COM, provide an active email address, and provide at least one other way to receive program alerts: either text message or phone number for voice messages (see Notifications below).
- You cannot enroll or remain in Prepaid Power if you have net energy metering; do not have a smart meter; do not live in the residence where prepaid service will be used; or if anyone in the household has a serious illness or uses life-supporting medical equipment that requires electricity. You must contact BGE immediately if these conditions occur during the program because you may need to be switched back to postpay service. If BGE determines you have or have developed the need for a medical certificate you will immediately be switched to post pay service.
- You cannot enroll in Prepaid Power if you have an arrearage of more than $600 unless you pay the outstanding balance down to $600 or less.
- BGE's Budget Billing and Autopay services are not offered in the Prepaid Program (though you may enroll in a new service called Auto Reload; see Enrollment and Payments).
- You must agree if contacted to participate in surveys before and after the program.
- While in the program, you will not be charged reconnection or late payment fees, or additional security deposits.
- Prepaid customers can return to postpay service at any time by notifying BGE.
- If you return to traditional postpay service during the pilot, you can only reenroll in Prepaid Power, following the requirements above, if the program's enrollment has not closed and if you pay all past-due amounts.
- After the 12-month pilot ends, you can choose to remain in Prepaid Power while the program is being evaluated. We will notify you if or when the program ends.

#### Enrollment and Payments

- Once you have enrolled and paid at least $40 into your account, you can use any of BGE's payment methods, though some payments take longer to post to your account than others (see Payment Methods list).
- For any payment methods that carry a $1.50 fee, BGE will reimburse the first one each month.
- If any payment is returned for insufficient funds, that payment amount will be deducted from your account balance immediately; a returned items fee of $15 will be charged to your account as part of the true-up process, as described in the Other Important Information section below. After two returned payments in a 12-month period you may not make further payments by personal check for another 12 months.
- Current customers with a past-due balance of $600 or less when they enroll in Prepaid Power may pay the amount due in total after enrolling, or in installments (see Deferred Balance Plan below).
- After you enroll you can sign up for auto-reload, where any time your account balance shows you have an estimated two days of service remaining, a payment will automatically be made for you in the amount you've chosen.
- If you are applying for new service, BGE's application fees apply (see the Consumer Reference Booklet), though no deposits are required for new customers who choose Prepaid Power service.

#### Deferred Balance Plan

- When you start Prepaid Power we will take any amount you owe, up to $600 – from an outstanding balance, or partial month charges we have not yet billed – and put them in a Deferred Balance Plan that you will be able to pay off over time. Any amount you owe greater than $600 must be paid before you enroll in Prepaid Power.
- With a Deferred Balance Plan, 25% of each new payment you make will be applied to the Deferred Balance until it is eliminated, and 75% will go into your Prepaid Power account balance for future energy use.
- Contact BGE after you make a payment if you want an additional amount to be applied toward your Deferred Balance.

#### Disconnection, Reconnection of Service

- You could be disconnected as soon as the next business day after your estimated account balance reaches zero. Note that your actual charges are only calculated once a month, and they could vary from the estimated daily prepaid charges; see the Other Important Information section for more information about this.
- BGE will not disconnect service outside regular weekday business hours, or during extreme weather periods, which means when temperatures are forecasted to remain below 32 degrees or above 95 degrees on at least one of the next three days.
- Given the restrictions on when service can be disconnected, your service could continue for a few days after the account has reached a zero balance. If so, we will notify you each day with the amount of this negative balance. To reconnect service you will need to pay any negative balance and bring your account balance to at least $15 (unless you call to ask for a five-day extension or to leave Prepaid Power service, as noted above).
- After your service has been disconnected you won't be charged for any gas or electricity use, but you will still be responsible for other utility charges (such as customer charges, taxes and fees), until your account is closed.
- Your account will close after your service has been disconnected for 20 business days (or sooner, if you request it) and you will receive a final bill. After that you must reapply for new service, and pay BGE's application fee (see the Consumer Reference Booklet).
- If you want to restart Prepaid Power, you must pay any negative balance you had, but not any amount in a Deferred Balance plan, which will be reestablished. No new security deposit will be required if you have not received a deposit refund.
- Customers with a retail supplier would need to contact them to restart their account.
- If you return to postpay service, any outstanding credit or debit, including a Deferred Balance, will be added to your first bill. Payment arrangements or assistance may be available.
- If you ask to stop your BGE service, BGE will issue a final bill for any amount due, or a refund check for any remaining credit balance.

#### Notifications

- It is your responsibility to maintain your contact methods, and your enrollment in BGE's online myAccount service, to receive BGE's notifications about account balance and estimated days of service remaining. You can access and review your account information online anytime.
- Please notify BGE if any of your contact methods change. You are responsible for any SMS/text message charges.
- We will notify you when we estimate that your account balance is sufficient for five, three and one days of service. You can go online to add more frequent notifications after you enroll in the program.
- Once you reach a zero-dollar balance, BGE will notify you that your service could be disconnected as early as the next business day. You must make sure you can receive the notifications so you can avoid service disconnections.
- You may select another person to receive a copy of any notifications you receive during the program. Let BGE know how to contact that person when you enroll.

#### Security Deposits

- Existing deposits will be refunded to Prepaid Power customers under similar rules for postpay customers. (See BGE's Consumer Reference Guide, page 3, as well as the Prepaid Frequently Asked Questions document for details.)
- New customers who choose Prepaid service will not be charged a deposit, but could be charged one at BGE's discretion if they switch to postpay before 12 months in Prepaid Power. If so, it would be charged on the first bill, and if more than $50 it may be paid in installments.

#### Safety and Other Risks of Service Disconnections

- Loss of gas or electric service can create health or safety risks, especially during extreme weather, and for very young or old residents, those with serious illnesses, or pets. Refrigerated foods and medications can spoil after even brief periods without power.
- Loss of utility service can be a breach of a rental or housing subsidy contract. Please consult your housing contract before enrolling in BGE's Prepaid Power.
- To avoid the risk of fire or damage to equipment, we strongly recommend that you unplug any large appliances, remove any flammable materials from heat sources, and unplug any sensitive electronics prior to the service being reestablished.

#### Assistance Funds

- An Assistance Agency grant commitment or pledge will not delay the disconnection of service until BGE receives the funds.
- Funds from the Maryland Office of Home Energy Programs (OHEP) will be applied first to any Deferred Balance plan, then to your Prepaid account for future service.
- OHEP EUSP Bill Assistance grants will be added to your prepaid account in 12 equal installments on the same date each month.
- OHEP MEAP grants will be added all at once to your account. BGE will always use OHEP funds first, and then any of your own funds you may have deposited into your account.
- If you choose Prepaid Power, you waive your right to obtain a 55-day extension that may be granted when postpay customers apply for OHEP assistance.

#### Other Important Information

- If you have chosen a retail supplier for gas or electricity, your choice to enroll in Prepaid Power will not change your supplier service or contract in any way.
- Customers with a retail supplier will have their daily Prepaid account balance charged with an estimate as if they were paying BGE's default service rate. But each month BGE will reconcile those charges with the actual charges we receive from your supplier, so you will always end up paying the supplier's price. See Supplier Customers Fact Sheet for additional important information.
- As noted above, BGE will deduct an estimate of charges and fees from your Prepaid balance each day. This will include both the estimated cost of the gas and/or electricity you used, plus a share of your monthly utility charges (such as customer charges, taxes and other fees). Your estimated daily Prepaid charges will be compared with the actual charges that are calculated each month, and any difference will be deducted from or credited to your Prepaid account. A credit will be applied immediately. Any debit (more than $1.00) will be spread over each of the next 30 days. This information will be available for you to see online.
- If we can't read your meter we will estimate your usage until the problem is fixed; if this lasts more than 14 days we will switch you back to postpay service. You must allow us access to your meters as a condition of participation in this program.
- If any charges transfer to your prepaid account from an old account they will be moved into the Deferred Balance Plan.
- If BGE determines you are responsible for fraud or theft charges during the program, we may at our discretion require you to pay those charges in full immediately. You have the right to dispute such charges directly with BGE, or with the Maryland Public Service Commission.
- By enrolling in the pilot program, you agree to allow BGE to share your account and usage information with our Prepaid vendor PayGo Utilities and program evaluator APPRISE (they will not share any personally identifiable information about you with anyone). Please see BGE's Privacy Policy for more information.

#### Changes to These Terms and Conditions

If we ever make changes to these terms and conditions we will let you know, and you can decide whether to stay in the program. Updates will be on our website, at BGE.COM/Prepaid.

## 1. Foreword

West Monroe Partners, LLC., (hereinafter referred as "West Monroe") was retained by BGE to evaluate the costs and benefits of BGE's participation in a formal, contractual agreement with the City of Baltimore that requires BGE to make capital improvements to the Baltimore City conduit system. This system and physical trench traverse the City, and contain electrical cable, manholes, fiber optic cable, and other infrastructure. It is a vital infrastructure for the region, and, importantly, BGE is required to locate its utility infrastructure within the conduit system within certain areas of the City.

For a period in 2025, a dedicated West Monroe team of evaluators worked alongside BGE subject matter experts to prepare the Benefit-Cost Analysis ("BCA") model and accompanying documentation. This effort incorporated actual data from 2023 and 2024, including recent conduit construction activities and enhancements to the Interruption Cost Estimate (ICE) 2.0 Calculator. West Monroe compared inputs to past practices and articulated plans from the City as the alternative scenario. The analysis identified the cost differences expected over the agreement period, along with the monetized and qualitative benefits accruing over the life of the improved conduit system to BGE's customers through the new arrangement.

This evaluation is supplemented and supported throughout with a detailed benefit-cost analysis (BCA) in MS Excel format which is available for review separately.

West Monroe prepared this evaluation pursuant to Maryland Public Service Commission (MD PSC) Order No. 90948 issued on December 14, 2023, in Case No. 9692.

## 2. Executive Summary

### Overview

This report identifies the costs and long-term benefits of a new agreement between the City of Baltimore and BGE to carry out essential capital improvements to the City's underground conduit system. This is referred to as the "2023 Agreement" or "New Agreement". This report describes the 2023 Agreement's features, and its benefits in comparison to the costs. It is based on a detailed review of the City's conduit work and BGE's activities to date, and it explains how the work is carried out in important new ways under the New Agreement. Overall, this report finds that the benefits of the 2023 Agreement exceed the costs when compared to the prior agreement on a present value (PV) basis, with a PV Benefit to Cost Ratio (BCR) of 1.15. This ratio demonstrates that the 2023 Agreement is not only cost-effective but delivers measurable benefits compared to the investment. It is furthermore expected that the Benefit to Cost Ratio will increase (from 1.15 to 1.21) the longer the New Agreement is maintained.

### Background

Subject to BGE demonstrating prudency in a future rate case proceeding based on a benefit cost analysis (BCA), the Maryland Public Service Commission's (MD PSC) MYP Order No. 90948 dated December 14, 2023 approved BGE's proposed capital and operating expenditure plan codified within the 2023 Agreement.

Under the New Agreement, BGE capitalizes conduit infrastructure improvements and reduces its maintenance payment compared to the prior agreement. Through this approach, BGE takes over responsibility from the City for specific conduit infrastructure investments and places that work within BGE's distribution system capital work planning and execution process.

Under the old agreement, the City executed its conduit work plan and was reimbursed for all conduit infrastructure and maintenance work through maintenance payments received from BGE and other tenants. BGE was on track to spend at least $300 million in O&M to the City over the 7-year period (2023-2029) had this New Agreement not been reached, and the City had the discretion to choose which work was undertaken.

The New Agreement has a duration of 7 years from 2023 to 2029. The parties have also agreed to the option of cancellation after 4 years under the New Agreement.

Figure 1 shows the timeline of the 2023 Agreement.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 1. The Timeline of the 2023 Agreement]

A horizontal timeline diagram spanning the years 2023 through 2029, illustrating the key milestones of the New Agreement: the one-time $14M maintenance payment and first tranche of capital improvements beginning in 2023, the $120M capital improvement phase covering 2023–2026, the option to cancel the agreement after year 4 (i.e., at the end of 2026), and the second capital improvement phase of $92M covering 2027–2029, alongside continuing annual maintenance payments of $1.5M from 2024 through 2029. The diagram visually emphasizes the two-phase capital spending structure and the mid-term cancellation option.

[→ See original PDF page 111 (Exhibit page 6) for visual rendering]

The following are the terms of the New Agreement:

- The 7-year term spans from 2023 to 2029, including $120M in capital improvements from 2023-2026 and $92M in capital improvements from 2027-2029.
- A one-time $14M maintenance payment in 2023 with annual maintenance payments of $1.5M scheduled from 2024 to 2029.

### Challenges Addressed by the 2023 Agreement

There were several challenges with the status quo leading up to the 2023 Agreement. The challenges can be summarized in terms of the following:

- Increasing payment levels over the last 20 years
- Spend misaligned to BGE customers' top priorities
- Steadily aging conduit
- Impacts on safety and reliability

The challenges, proposed solutions, and impacts are articulated in the exhibit below:

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 2. Status Quo Challenges Addressed through the 2023 Agreement Solutions]

A structured exhibit (likely a table-style graphic with three columns: Challenge, Solution, Impact) pairing each of the four status-quo challenges — rising payment levels, spend misalignment with BGE customer priorities, aging conduit infrastructure, and safety/reliability impacts — with the corresponding feature of the 2023 Agreement that addresses it (e.g., shifting to BGE-capitalized, BGE-directed capital work; reducing ongoing maintenance payments; prioritizing electric-system-relevant work; and proactive full-section replacement practices) and the expected positive outcome of each solution.

[→ See original PDF page 112 (Exhibit page 7) for visual rendering]

### Expected Outcomes of the 2023 Agreement Compared to the As-If Scenario

The 2023 Agreement endeavored to achieve the following outcomes versus the old agreement.

- **Financial Benefits to Customers.** BGE was on track to spend at least $300 million in O&M to the City over the 7-year period (2023-2029) had this new agreement not been reached. By comparison, the New Agreement (2023-2029) represents $212 million in Capital infrastructure improvement and $23 million in O&M to the City for ongoing maintenance in total. This saves customers a significant amount of money in the short term because O&M is always paid upfront. In the long-term, the comparative spend profile is approximately neutral from a present value perspective, with both spend profiles costing $274 million.

- **Alignment of Spend to Match BGE Customer Needs.** Under the old agreement, approximately 35% of the City's work pertaining to the conduit was not aligned to areas of work that BGE would prioritize for the benefit of its electric customers (roughly $100 million in funds from BGE O&M to the City). This work pertained to Department of Transportation infrastructure (e.g., highway and bridge work), certain "streetscape" enhancements, performing repairs over installing replacements, locking manholes, making inspections, and other overhead costs. Under the New Agreement, all capital work is 100% aligned to the service needs of BGE customers. Furthermore, BGE historically expended additional monies annually to build new conduit infrastructure for BGE project work that would be turned over to the City upon completion that is now accounted for directly ($19 million over 7 years).

- **Improved Conduit Infrastructure.** Today, much of the conduit infrastructure is over 100 years old with outdated and eroded materials. Some of this erosion triggers emergencies and high priority responsive work from the City. However, some of the erosion is not found until work is needed that discovers the conditions. It is estimated that over 35% of the ducts and cables are currently obstructed in place.

  Historically, the City has repaired conduit sections found in an eroded condition; however; it is BGE's philosophy to replace whole duct sections with new/larger diameter materials while also laying parallel duct work for a variety of purposes (making duct available for future BGE occupancy, shortening future outage restoration by having additional duct capacity, and taking advantage of fixed costs by placing parallel duct work at negligible incremental cost levels). The same is done for new and larger manhole rebuilds that are safer for working conditions. This way of working creates a positive feedback loop that gives stakeholders a better chance of seeing the aging infrastructure continuously addressed rather than losing a battle against time. The result is fewer obstructions in the future where work is performed by BGE, safer working conditions through expanded infrastructure, and a better long-term asset-health trajectory supporting customer reliability.

Through BGE's ways of working during the 7-year agreement period and through the long-term impacts on conduit health, roughly $40 million additional present value will be generated to customers, mainly driven by reliability benefits calculated using outputs from the Department of Energy's ICE 2.0 Calculator, which is a framework used and accepted in multiple proceedings at the MD PSC. This is the result when comparing the net benefits and costs of the new agreement to what they would have been under the old agreement. Conclusively, there are greater present value benefits over costs, highlighting the long-term beneficial nature of this 7-year period of work from 2023 to 2029.

### Results

From a cost standpoint, the 2023 Agreement was defined by BGE's work activities planned (2023-2029) while the As-If scenario represents what BGE's spending and contribution would have been had the old agreement remained in place.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 4. Nominal BGE Cost Comparison]

A bar or line chart comparing nominal (undiscounted) BGE costs — likely split between Capital and O&M spending — under the 2023 Agreement versus the As-If scenario, plotted across the 2023-2029 agreement period. The chart is designed to visually contrast the two spending profiles, showing the New Agreement's shift toward capitalized spend (paid off gradually) against the As-If scenario's continued reliance on upfront O&M maintenance payments to the City.

[→ See original PDF page 114 (Exhibit page 9) for visual rendering]

[Note: The source figure numbering skips from "Figure 2" to "Figure 4" with no "Figure 3" appearing in the extracted text between them; this may reflect a figure that was renumbered or removed in the source, or an extraction gap. [sic]]

When these Capital and O&M costs are passed into a revenue requirement model to approximate the impact to customer rates (i.e., applying the Rate Impact Measure (or "RIM") test), the present value of that spend profile across both scenarios is approximately equal to $274 million. However, the 2023 Agreement results in significantly less paid by customers upfront due to capital being paid off gradually as time goes on. This outcome highlights the positive outcome to customers not having to pay the much higher operating costs of the As-If scenario all at once.

To incorporate quantifiable reliability benefits and be able to complete the Benefit Cost Analysis directed by the MD PSC, a ratio was determined focusing on net benefits compared to costs. Net benefits were defined as avoided costs of the As-If scenario and the incremental reliability benefits generated by the 2023 Agreement beyond that which would have come from the City's work. For costs that remain, these were just the costs of the 2023 Agreement. This comparison was done in terms of present value costs to customers from a bill impact perspective, as expressed through a revenue requirement model. This result is ($274M As-If Costs Avoided + $40M New Agreement Reliability Benefit) / ($274M Costs of the New Agreement) for a Ratio of 1.15.

If the comparison were in terms of purely Capital and O&M, it would be a much more positive business case. Application of the Utility Cost Test resulted in a Benefit-to-Cost Ratio of 1.35. The high-level calculations and results of all three tests are summarized in the figure below.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 5. Cost Test from the Customer Perspective (Present Value Revenue Requirement & Reliability)]

A summary exhibit (likely a table or set of side-by-side panels) presenting the high-level calculations and results of the three cost-effectiveness tests discussed in the report: the RIM test with reliability benefits (BCR of 1.15), a Capital & O&M-only comparison, and the Utility Cost Test (BCR of 1.35). Each panel likely shows the numerator (benefits/avoided costs) and denominator (costs of the New Agreement) feeding into the respective ratio, framed from the customer bill-impact perspective.

[→ See original PDF page 115 (Exhibit page 10) for visual rendering]

West Monroe ultimately recommends the RIM test with reliability benefits as the most appropriate test for purposes of this report. This approach incorporates both quantifiable costs and benefits from a net present value perspective. This business case stands out because, unlike many others that involve higher upfront costs with benefits accruing gradually over time, the 2023 Agreement delivers immediate value. The bulk of benefits are realized right away through immediate cost savings coupled with a high degree of reliability benefit realization through the efficiencies of BGE's execution. Additionally, the benefit-to-cost ratio is expected to grow over time (from 1.15 to 1.21) if this approach is sustained, as ongoing infrastructure improvements create a compounding effect of increasing benefits. The potential for even greater long-term value is examined later in this report, showcasing how maintaining this approach could amplify benefits and deliver even more impactful outcomes for stakeholders.

### Summary

The BCA demonstrates a favorable outcome, with a 1.15 Benefit to Cost Ratio based on the present value of customer revenue requirement impacts and reliability improvements. Over the 7-year term of the 2023 Agreement, the work will deliver significant positive outcomes, including enhanced conduit infrastructure health and improved safety. This agreement represents a win-win for all stakeholders: the City gains flexibility and funding to focus on other priorities, while BGE and its customers benefit from electric infrastructure upgrades and reliability improvements. Moreover, the benefit-cost ratio is expected to increase over time if this type of agreement is sustained, as the immediate benefits from work delivery are complemented by long-term, compounding benefits that accumulate over time.

## 3. Introduction

### 3.1 Background

#### Background on the Conduit

The City of Baltimore owns and has historically managed a conduit that traverses the City and contains electric and fiber optic communication cables within separate shielded pipes. The pipes making up the conduit represent millions of duct-miles that are occupied by various tenants running electric cable, fiber optic, telecom, and other services underground and winding through Baltimore City.

The conduit is installed underground between manholes, buildings, and structures to allow for electric and communication cables to connect homes and businesses. The conduit manholes serve as access points to service the existing cables and install new cable within the ducts, allowing for the replacement and repair of existing cables with limited costs when the infrastructure is healthy with sufficient capacity.

The electric and other cables provide essential services to Baltimore's residents and businesses. The system supports electricity for homes, businesses, and even traffic lights. Therefore, it is important that the conduit is well maintained, upgraded, and expanded to meet the needs of the community.

The conduit is very old, with many of its miles dating back to the early 1900s when it was first constructed. As a result, maintenance and upgrade work is continuously required. When the conduit walls degrade or even collapse, or separate lengths of conduit sections break down in an observable way, work must occur immediately to provide service.

Baltimore City recognizes the role that the conduit system provides to its businesses and residences, as electrical power is delivered to them through BGE's underground power lines located in the conduit system. In addition to electrical power, the conduit system is necessary to support the increasing demand for data and communication. The City is committed to working with BGE to ensure that infrastructure needs are met and to collaborate on prioritization.

#### Old and New Lease Agreements

While Baltimore owns the conduit system, it leases out access to BGE and other occupants, including several communication companies. BGE is the largest user of the Conduit (over 75% of the physical space/duct-miles) and has historically paid the highest level of fees. Additionally, the costs of the lease agreement were steadily increasing over time as shown below.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 7. Historic Cost Trend of Required Conduit Rent Payments]

A line or bar chart showing the historical trend of BGE's required conduit rent/maintenance payments to the City of Baltimore over roughly the last 20 years, illustrating a generally upward trajectory in payment levels. This chart supports the report's framing of "increasing payment levels over the last 20 years" as one of the key challenges addressed by the 2023 Agreement.

[→ See original PDF page 122 (Exhibit page 13) for visual rendering]

The lease agreement between the City and BGE expired in June 2022. Under this prior agreement, BGE paid maintenance fees to Baltimore City that in turn covered the costs of crews repairing and upgrading the conduit infrastructure. However, the City and BGE determined that there is a better way to set up a new agreement. Instead of the City conducting all infrastructure work and BGE paying maintenance fees that were increasing to a prospective $300 million over 7 years, the new agreement requires BGE to carry out and pay for much of the capital improvement work to ensure readiness for critical infrastructure, with a specific level of investment required:

- During the 4 years from 2023-2026:
  - Capital improvements of $120M (Capital) are planned.
  - A specific $14M (O&M) maintenance payment is allocated for 2023.
  - Annual maintenance payments of $1.5M (O&M) are scheduled for 2024-2026.
- During the 3 years from 2027-2029:
  - Additional capital improvements of $92M (Capital) are planned.
  - Annual maintenance payments of $1.5M (O&M) will continue for 2027-2029.

Beyond paying the maintenance fees under the old agreement in the "As-If" scenario, BGE was often required to spend several million dollars additionally each year to build new conduit for project work.[^jcf3-fn1] Based on its actual spending trends, BGE estimated it would supplement an additional $19 million in separate conduit capital work alongside the City's efforts. Given the prospective payments BGE was facing of $300 million over 7 years, the total "As-If" scenario is represented by $319 million (2023-2029) had this new 2023 Agreement not been reached.

[^jcf3-fn1]: BGE is required to turn over the new conduit to Baltimore City.

Under the New Agreement, BGE has the discretion to determine what work should be carried out on a day by day, week by week and month by month basis. On behalf of its customers, BGE prefers this approach because it takes charge of the work 'part and parcel' of all its other electric grid activities, which of course are aimed to keep the electric system well-functioning.

Through comprehensive long-term planning, BGE strives to ensure the conduit capital improvement work receives the right level of priority and that this work is aligned with BGE's plans for upgrading the electric system. This is work that is foundational to enabling increased building electrification and electric vehicle utilization.

Nominal spend by BGE under the 2023 Agreement versus the As-If Agreement in shown graphically and in numerical form in the table below.

[→ See original PDF page 123 (Exhibit page 14) for visual rendering]

As shown and described above, under the New Agreement BGE will direct and manage the work, and pay reduced maintenance fees, for a reduced total cost of $235 million. When this cost makes it through the process of charging electric customers on their utility bills, it is approximately equal to the old contract ($274 million), on present value terms.[^jcf3-en-i] Yet, because of work practices and how it manages priorities, BGE estimates that under the new agreement it will complete more conduit capital improvement work and create greater reliability benefits for customers. These observations are based on a detailed review of the work plans and activity levels under both agreements.

### 3.2 Stakeholder/Maryland PSC Questions

In 2023, the City of Baltimore and BGE came to the new agreement described above. The MD PSC expressed concerns about ensuring that the outcomes of the new agreement were not just for the benefit of the utility and that costs were equitable, in summary.

Because of the conduit's importance and the impact of a shift in dollars toward Capital from O&M that is recovered over time through customer rates, the MD PSC requested that BGE answer several questions about the new agreement compared to the old. At its root, the MD PSC asks whether the new agreement benefits exceed the costs when compared to the old agreement for BGE's customers. In further support of this main question, it also asks for BGE to identify all costs of the new agreement and to quantify reliability benefits[^jcf3-en-ii] related to the conduit infrastructure work.

These questions are summarized in the Call out Box.[^jcf3-en-iii] This report addresses each of these questions. It relies on the supporting analysis noted earlier, and the underlying calculations are available in MS Excel format.

### 3.3 Key Information Sources Defining Activities

#### Information Sources to Estimate City Activities, Costs, and Outcomes

West Monroe, when creating the BCA, conducted workshops with various functional groups from BGE, such as construction & maintenance, electric operations, reliability & programs, regulatory, and legal to gain perspective from the BGE subject matter experts to interpret information correctly and ensure the BCA was utilizing accurate inputs.

A core part of the benefit cost analysis was to estimate the City's activities and costs under the prior agreement, which expired in 2022. Since the benefit cost analysis compares all the planned work activities under the old and new agreements, West Monroe needed to specify the City's work activities and costs "As-If" the old agreement remained in place. To accomplish this, West Monroe utilized detailed budgets, work activities, and intended outcomes per activity that were previously provided by the City to BGE.

To derive estimates of the work and related costs, West Monroe used several information sources:

- Baltimore City's completed work for the period 2017-2021.
- The City's conduit plan for 2022-2026 (consistent levels applied through 2029).
- Conduit obstruction data for 2017-2023.
- Electrical fault event data for 2018-2022.

#### Specific Projects, Obstructions, and Manhole Work Activities

**Projects:**

The photo below is an example of a conduit rebuild. There are several types of project work described in the BGE and City plans that each serve a unique purpose.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 9. Newly Installed Conduit with Temporary Shoring]

A construction-site photograph showing a section of newly installed underground conduit with temporary shoring (structural bracing) in place to support the excavation walls during installation. [→ See original PDF page 127 (Exhibit page 18) for visual rendering]

**BGE's Project Activity Categorization:**

- **4kV** – A portion of BGE's customers in Baltimore City receive power from 4kV distribution lines. The new duct segments decrease the average age of the conduit system and mitigate potential failures, while the parallel duct allows for faster outage restoration and avoids digging again for new services.
- **Amtrak** – This project activity is needed to continue the necessary upgrades on the signal system that supports Amtrak operations and allows for better planning with respect to other conduit upgrades.
- **Reliability/System Planning** - The new installation of conduit leads to an improved average asset age, increase in conduit capacity, and a decrease in restored outage duration. Targeted projects with respect to the reliability and system planning initiatives allow for synergies across BGE departments.
- **Substation** - Substations are vital assets in BGE's electric grid. If the conduit system that supports these substations fail, there could be cascading effects to power delivery. New installations ensure that the conduit system is updated to support this critical infrastructure.

**The City's Project Activity Categorization:**

- **Department of Transportation (DOT) Infrastructure** - Upgrades to the conduit system in support of highways, bridges, and other DOT infrastructure are not directly related to electricity delivery and periodically require outages.
- **Parallel Duct Bank**: Improvements to the conduit system for parallel duct banks allow for easier installation of additional duct segments and enables minimal distribution to customers by leveraging ongoing construction during streetscape projects.
- **Split Duct Bank**: Split duct bank work allows for alignment of conduit construction with ongoing City infrastructure improvements, improves conduit resiliency, and decreases the asset age of the conduit infrastructure. The work also increases efficiency of streetscape work.

**Obstructions**

Looking at the photo below, one can see that the joints in these terra-cotta ducts are separating and causing obstructions, making it extremely difficult to remove existing cables. Improvements are required to these ducts, which were installed 100+ years ago. Repairing is not enough, replacements are needed.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 10. Terra-Cotta Requiring Repairs]

A close-up photograph of aging terra-cotta conduit ducts showing separated joints and material degradation, illustrating the physical condition that causes cable-removal obstructions and necessitates full-section replacement rather than repair.

[→ See original PDF page 129 (Exhibit page 20) for visual rendering]

**BGE's Obstruction Activity Categorization:**

- **Amtrak**: This project activity is needed to maintain functionality and upgrades to new tunnels and boring equipment supporting Amtrak operations.
- **Contact Voltage (CV)**: When the surrounding environment causes a fault in the Conduit, the electricity can be unintentionally dissipated to the duct. This causes dangerous conditions for workers and loss of electricity for customers.
- **Underground Lines (UGL)**: The underground lines are the core power delivery lines for BGE. This initiative provides a large benefit across outage restoration duration, average asset age, conduit capacity, and other service reliability due to the number of customers impacted in this category.
- **Streetlight (ODL)**: The conduit improvements for the streetlights enable more consistent lighting throughout the City which improves Baltimore City residents' and visitors' experience and adds value to other reliability.
- **Fiber-Optic (FC)**: Fiber-optic focused conduit improvements enable better communication, which benefits both electric grid controls and other telecommunications needs of other conduit occupants.

**The City's Obstruction Activity Categorization:**

- **Cable Racking**: There are safety risks associated with unorganized ducts and manholes; however, these can be mitigated with relevant industry best practices when conducting other project work.
- **Rodding, Slugging, and Roping**: These efforts enable more efficient clearing of obstructions; however, this can also be accounted for in the initial steps of an obstruction clearance.

**Manholes**

Observing the photos below, the top two are both examples of overcrowded manholes. Overcrowded manholes can be dangerous since they can lead to increased risks of fires due to overheating as a result of too many wires in a confined space and limitation of airflow. There is compromised access for maintenance as the spaces are already extremely tight. Adding more wires does not help and makes it hard for electric cable repairs to be made safely, causing service disruptions to last longer. This also increases the safety hazards for workers since they are more likely to interact with a live cable. Due to all these reasons, BGE is committed to making new manholes and improving efficiency, as seen in the bottom two photos.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 11. Overcrowded Manholes w/ 360 Scan, Transitioned with Manhole Re-build, or New with Pre-cast Manholes]

A four-photo composite: the top two photos show overcrowded manholes with dense, disorganized cabling and a "360 scan" documentation approach; the bottom two photos show the result after a manhole rebuild or new pre-cast manhole installation, with organized, higher-capacity cable racking and improved working clearance.

[→ See original PDF page 130 (Exhibit page 21) for visual rendering]

**BGE's Manhole Activity Categorization:**

- **Manhole Rebuild/Reconstruct**: Manholes serve as the only access points to the conduit system without requiring ground construction. These spaces are critical for conduit tenants and City workers but are often constrained by poor working conditions and fire risks. Increasing manhole size improves both safety and working conditions.

**The City's Manhole Activity Categorization:**

- **Manhole Repairs**: Repairs address constrained working conditions within manholes, which are essential access points for conduit maintenance.
- **Locking Manholes**: Locking manholes prevents unauthorized access to restricted infrastructure but can reduce response efficiency during repairs.
- **Manhole/Conduit Occupation & Inspections**: Regular inspections improve safety within the system, even though they do not directly enhance reliability.

## 4. Methodology

### 4.1 Purpose and Scope of the BCA

The MD PSC MYP Order No. 90948 approved BGE's proposed capital and operating expense plan for the new Agreement while also requiring that a BCA be performed. The New Agreement's term expires in 2029 with a possible option to reduce it to a 4-year term. The MD PSC found that it is unclear whether the new agreement will benefit ratepayers or be a burden to them inclusive of future costs.

### 4.2 Performing a BCA

In response to the MD PSC's directive, BGE engaged West Monroe to perform a benefit cost analysis of the new conduit agreement to address the MD PSC's questions. To do so, West Monroe gathered estimates of the costs of the new and old agreements and modeled the impact to infrastructure and reliability over a full depreciation timeline of 50+ years. It also identified avoided costs and other benefits of the new agreement when compared to the old in terms of infrastructure differences, frequency of obstruction events, and reliability. For reliability specifically, West Monroe placed an estimate of financial value on customer minutes interrupted (CMI).

Budgeted activities lead to impacts to infrastructure and reliability:

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 14. Activity Level Build Up to Infrastructure Outcomes and Reliability Benefits]

A flow diagram showing how granular, activity-level work inputs (by project/obstruction/manhole subcategory) aggregate upward into infrastructure-level outcomes (asset age, duct capacity, obstruction rates) and ultimately into reliability benefits (reduced outage minutes and their financial value).

[→ See original PDF page 133 (Exhibit page 24) for visual rendering]

The BCA Methodology again used the outage minute benefit as inputs for step one in a four-step process with the following logic: 1. Activities and execution difference yield different infrastructure and outage minutes across the scenarios being compared, 2. Reduced outage minutes lead to Reliability Benefits, 3. Capital and O&M was passed through a Revenue Requirements calculation, and 4. Benefits and Costs were compared to produce a Benefit to Cost Ratio (BCR).

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 15. Structured methodology to evaluate benefits and costs of the two agreement scenarios]

A four-step process flow diagram illustrating the BCA methodology: (1) Activity/execution differences → different infrastructure and outage minutes; (2) Reduced outage minutes → Reliability Benefits; (3) Capital and O&M → Revenue Requirements calculation; (4) Benefits and Costs compared → Benefit to Cost Ratio (BCR). Each step is likely shown as a labeled box connected by arrows to the next.

[→ See original PDF page 134 (Exhibit page 25) for visual rendering]

With all the estimated costs and calculated financial benefits lined up by year, it was possible to compare the new and old agreements and calculate the differences. The result is the difference between the new and old agreements as conceptually illustrated below.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 16. Benefit Cost Comparison]

A conceptual chart (likely a waterfall or side-by-side bar comparison) illustrating how the New Agreement's costs and benefits compare against the old ("As-If") agreement's costs and benefits, visually decomposing the net benefit into avoided-cost and reliability-benefit components versus the New Agreement's cost.

[→ See original PDF page 135 (Exhibit page 26) for visual rendering]

The results of the benefit cost analysis are provided again later in this report. To help make the results easier to understand, the results are expressed as a ratio. The numerator (net benefits) includes the avoided costs of the old agreement plus the incremental reliability improvement expected through the New Agreement. The denominator (costs) consists of the New Agreement costs. If the benefit/cost ratio is greater than 1.0, we estimate that the benefits of the new agreement exceed its costs.[^jcf3-en-iv]

### 4.3 Cost and Benefit Inventory

A benefit cost analysis should begin with a comprehensive inventory of associated costs and benefits. Wherever possible, each cost and benefit should be assigned a monetary value. If assigning a financial value is not feasible, qualitative descriptions should be provided to capture their significance. Qualitative benefits, while harder to quantify, remain highly relevant to the overall analysis. For instance, safety improvements are often difficult to express in financial terms but are critical outcomes. This report includes both quantitative and qualitative outcomes, highlighting the numerous advantages of the new agreement compared to the previous one.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 17. Cost and Benefit Inventory Composition Diagram]

A composition diagram (likely a two-column or matrix layout) inventorying the categories of costs and benefits considered in the BCA, distinguishing quantitative (monetized) items — such as capital/O&M costs and reliability minutes avoided — from qualitative items such as safety improvements, economic development, and greenhouse gas reduction.

[→ See original PDF page 137 (Exhibit page 28) for visual rendering]

### 4.4 Estimating Reliability Improvements

An important benefit of the 2023 Agreement when compared to the old agreement is improvements to electrical system reliability. West Monroe leveraged the Department of Energy's ICE 2.0 Calculator to estimate the cost of minutes of service interruption in Baltimore City based on the customer mix, current reliability levels, and levels of energy/demand to work up to a $/minute impact when service is interrupted. This tool is widely used by utilities and state commissions to value Reliability improvements and has been accepted in multiple proceedings at the Maryland PSC.

Conceptually, this value of avoided service interruption is generally higher than the basic service rate (as expressed in $/kWh) of electricity for several reasons. Good reliability means that the electrical system is performing well with minimal levels of electrical outages as measured in terms of number of outage events, their duration, and their extent. The principle at work is this: if a customer does not have electrical service this is not simply an inconvenience. That customer faces a host of difficulties and hazards depending on their circumstances. Will their business be able to open its doors? Will the residents lose perishable food or face tripping and other hazards in their home, or will a sump pump fail during heavy rains? Because of the immediacy of these concerns, economists have found effective ways to translate the reliability shortfall into its economic value equivalent. These methods rely on customer surveys that in turn estimate the value that customers of all sizes perceive when the power is out.

Under the 2023 Agreement, BGE estimates that it will lower total outage minutes by reducing the number of separate outage events and the time it takes to restore service. The reason for the favorable estimate under the 2023 Agreement is that BGE gets work done more quickly (by several hours for most events, for both planned and unplanned work), and this reduces total outage time. Also, BGE installs more new conduit footage, and this will shorten the length of future outages.

To demonstrate the impact to outage minutes in aggregate across all activity types, a different profile of planned and responsive outages during the agreement term and over the life of the infrastructure was constructed. This is exemplified in the visuals below.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 18. Influenceable Outage Minutes - Planned vs. Responsive 2023 Agreement]

A chart (likely stacked bar or area chart over time) breaking down "influenceable" customer outage minutes into planned versus responsive (unplanned) categories under the 2023 Agreement, showing the profile of avoided outage minutes across the analysis period.

[→ See original PDF page 139 (Exhibit page 30, per running footer — note: this figure appears on the page numbered 29 in the exhibit's own pagination) for visual rendering]

The basis of the outage time assumption per work activity under the 2023 Agreement is input from BGE subject matter experts about BGE's work approaches combined with looking at actual experience over the past year. For the As-If scenario outage time assumptions, West Monroe worked with BGE subject matter experts to assess the average times to conduct work under the old and new agreements, applied these durations to the work activity estimates and confirmed the bottom-up results aligned with historic conduit reliability over past years. The BCA also assumes that the outages have the potential to impact residential, commercial, and industrial customers, based on the average percentage of these customer types on any given circuit. When calculated, most of the reliability benefits (nearly 80%) are created during the initial 7-year term because of BGE's execution strengths while the remaining benefit takes place over the full timeline as a result of the improved infrastructure. West Monroe estimates that under the 2023 Agreement period (2023-2029) over 30 million planned and unplanned customer minutes of interruption will be avoided and that even more minutes will be saved over time. It is also worth noting again that, should the 2023 Agreement continue past the initial 7-year term, the reliability benefits will continue to accumulate.

## 5. Findings and Analysis

### 5.1 Infrastructure Drivers of Benefits

The BCA concludes that the 2023 Agreement is beneficial for Baltimore City, BGE customers, and the wider community, as compared to the alternative. The BCA is also consistent with prior BGE testimonies while elaborating (with significant new detail) on costs and benefits. Main takeaways from the BCA conclude a favorable BCA, additional benefits, win-win results, and long-term upside potential. The 2023 Agreement provides qualitative and quantitative benefits, including reliability, safety, and asset health improvements that further improve the favorable outlook. It is also a win-win result that provides the City with more flexibility, as City leaders can focus their efforts on other priorities beneficial to the City.

The 2023 Agreement also results in more long-term improvements to the conduit system. This includes:

**Table 3. Infrastructure Difference Summary**

| Infrastructure            | Total   | Yearly Avg |
| ------------------------- | ------- | ---------- |
| Replaced Footage          | 92,700  | 13,200     |
| Parallel Footage          | 503,700 | 72,000     |
| Net New Manholes          | 35      | 5          |
| Replaced/Reconstructed MH | 23      | 3          |

Additionally, and specifically with regards to reliability and system performance, reliability through fewer and reduced outages is expected to be improved under the 2023 Agreement. The value of this reduction in outage minutes is $40 million. And outage minutes are reduced by over 30 million minutes within the first 7 years.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 19. Improvement benefits long term from the single 7-year period of work]

A chart illustrating how the reliability and infrastructure-health benefits generated during the single 7-year (2023-2029) period of concentrated work continue to accrue and compound over the full, longer-term life of the infrastructure (beyond 2029), visually distinguishing the initial-term benefit from the long-tail benefit.

[→ See original PDF page 140 (Exhibit page 30) for visual rendering]

### 5.2 Benefit Cost Analysis Results

#### 2023 Agreement Benefits

Benefits shown below are summarized in terms of Financial, Qualitative, and Drivers:

**Benefits of the New Agreement: Financial Benefits That Have Been Calculated**

- Neutral present value of costs to customers ($274 million for both As-If and 2023 Agreement but more work planned and completed with enhanced outcomes under the New Agreement).[^jcf3-en-v]
- Fewer electrical outages (reliability benefits estimated at $40 million, on a present value basis)

**Benefit to Cost Ratios**

- **Utility Cost Test (UCT)**: This test evaluates the financial impact of the 2023 Agreement from the utility's perspective. The UCT compares the avoided costs or benefits of $268M (present value of "as-if" costs, including capital and O&M) to the $198M cost of the 2023 Agreement, resulting in a 1.35 Benefit-to-Cost Ratio (BCR), demonstrating that the 2023 Agreement is cost-effective for BGE.
- **Rate Impact Measure (RIM)**: This test assesses the impact of the 2023 Agreement on customer rates. The RIM compares the $274M of "as-if" costs to $274M in 2023 Agreement costs, yielding a neutral 1.0 BCR, showing that capitalization of investments saves customers on rates for every year the 2023 Agreement is in effect and that the recovery of capital costs over time is equitable based on the 1.0 result in present value terms. RIM with Reliability: Incorporating the $40M reliability benefit into the RIM analysis results in a 1.15 BCR, which highlights that the Agreement provides value beyond financial savings.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 20. Cost Test from the Customer Perspective (Present Value Revenue Requirement & Reliability)]

A repeat/updated version of the Executive Summary's Figure 5, summarizing the RIM, RIM-with-Reliability, and Utility Cost Test calculations and resulting BCRs (1.0, 1.15, and 1.35 respectively) from the customer bill-impact perspective.

[→ See original PDF page 141 (Exhibit page 32) for visual rendering]

**Qualitative Benefits**

West Monroe believes it is prudent to express several 2023 Agreement benefits as qualitative. As observable from the list below, some benefits include a quantified measure (such as feet replaced), which adds additional perspective for that benefit. There are several and unique reasons (depending on the benefit) that these benefits are treated as qualitative. Identified benefits, also, are all economic in nature.

- Lower long-term maintenance and capital costs due to lower repairs by building to higher standards
- Improved safety due to building with higher standards
- Improved economic activity by serving new load requirements, and new services like fiber, more quickly
- Higher levels of electric system resiliency, which means fewer economic losses that are caused by outages and failures
- Reduced greenhouse gas emissions due to fewer truck trips, reduced traffic disruptions and faster conduction of work

**What Causes the Benefits?**

The drivers behind these economic benefits include:

- More replacement of original terracotta pipe by 2030
- Work prioritization leads to supporting new electrical loads, like electric vehicles, more quickly
- Conduit is upgraded to a higher standard (materials and design choices)
- Fewer conduit obstructions are estimated in the future (which cause fewer and shorter outages impacting City residents and businesses)
- Lower maintenance costs expected long term
- Increased safety due to better materials and larger parallel duct and manhole diameters
- The conduit is better prepared for expansion for fiber and other uses

### 5.3 Continuation Scenario

Continuing this way of working indefinitely, benefits stack for greater results than the 7 years that were primarily analyzed within this report. Benefits of continued capital spending include improved revenue requirement present value, increased reliability benefit (as conduit health improves), better responsive time on obstruction work, and greater ability to meet future demands including for electrification. While the 7 years of work add value to Baltimore and its residents, a return to status quo erodes from that value and prevents the peaks of value that could otherwise be realized.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 21. Cumulative NPV Benefits by Year]

A cumulative line chart showing net present value (NPV) benefits accumulating year by year, comparing a scenario where the 2023 Agreement's way of working continues indefinitely against one limited to the initial 7-year term, illustrating the additional ~$177 million in cumulative NPV benefit available under indefinite continuation.

[→ See original PDF page 143 (Exhibit page 33) for visual rendering]

This outlook demonstrates the expectation that the benefit to cost ratio will increase the longer this protocol is maintained and not cut-off due to the feedback loop of improving infrastructure and stacking long duration benefit tails. In fact, an indefinite execution of this arrangement is estimated to create over an additional $177 million in present value (for a total PV of $217 million) and increase the benefit to cost ratio further to 1.21. Though the focus of this BCA is on the 7-year agreement term, this highlights the potential to further amplify results by enabling BGE to execute in this fashion while encouraging opportunities for further cost efficiency and prioritization to maximize value per dollar spent on the conduit relative to the previous status-quo.

## 6. Discussions & Conclusions

In Order No. 90948, the MD PSC issued the following directive:

> "[T]he Commission will authorize BGE's proposed expenditures associated with the new Baltimore City Conduit agreement, but the Commission will require that BGE provide a benefit cost analysis consistent with the recommendation and the parameters provided by Staff witness Dererie. Based on that analysis, BGE will be subject to a prudency review at the reconciliation stage of this MYP. BGE will have the burden of demonstrating the prudency of entering into the new agreement with Baltimore City."[^jcf3-fn2]

[^jcf3-fn2]: Case No. 9692, Order No. 90948, page 102.

In response to this directive, BGE engaged West Monroe to perform a BCA of the new conduit agreement to address any concerns. After a thorough investigation and analysis, where West Monroe met with multiple stakeholders, performed reliability calculations, and reviewed revenue requirements, it was concluded that the 2023 Agreement brings the City of Baltimore and BGE significant benefits relative to cost.

With the 2023 Agreement, BGE produces the expected outcomes included in Figure 22 below.

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 22. Summary of Challenges Being Addressed through 2023 Agreement]

A recap exhibit (echoing Figure 2 from the Executive Summary) summarizing how each of the original status-quo challenges — rising payments, spend misalignment, aging conduit, and safety/reliability impacts — is addressed by an outcome of the 2023 Agreement.

[→ See original PDF page 144 (Exhibit page 34) for visual rendering]

Furthermore, the quantitative takes in the results section above are summarized as follows:

- More one-way miles of conduit replaced and upgraded
- Lower outage minutes by reducing the number of separate outage events and time it takes to restore service
- During the 7 years between 2023-2029, over $40 million additional present value customer benefit will be generated, resulting in a Benefit to Cost Ratio of 1.15
- Over $177M more cumulative NPV benefits if maintaining this way of working indefinitely into the future, increasing the Benefit to Cost Ratio to 1.21

[DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION: Figure 23. Summary of Results]

A final capstone summary exhibit (similar to Figure 6 in the Executive Summary) restating the headline BCR figures (1.15 growing to 1.21), the $40 million reliability benefit, and the $177 million additional cumulative benefit from indefinite continuation.

[→ See original PDF page 145 (Exhibit page 35) for visual rendering]

The 2023 Agreement is a win for Baltimore City and the community. Everyone benefits when the Conduit is improved cost-effectively to reduce outage events and improve safety, keeping the City attractive for businesses (which benefit the City and the broader region).
